24 July 1995

Kozyrev Attends ‘Contact Group’ Meeting on
Bosnia
Opposes ‘Massive’ Air Strikes

LD2207105895 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1028 GMT 22 Jul 95

{By Yuriy Kozlov and Igor Polskiy]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] London July 22 (ITAR-TASS)
— The proposal of massive air strikes on Bosnian Serb
positions near Gorazde and other Moslem enclaves did
not find general support at the international meeting on
Bosnia which finished in London on Friday, Russian
Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev told a news confer-
ence.

Recounting results of the meeting, Kozyrev said the
Russian delegation expressed opposition to the use of
force which is fraught with making UN peacekeeping
forces a party in the conflict.

A lack of consensus on military aspects of the Bosnia
problem prevented the meeting participants from pass-
ing a final document.

However, Kozyrev said, the London meeting was an
important international event which allowed Western
powers to compare their stances and mobilize efforts to
recruit the potential of peaceful measures for settlement.
This poteniial is far from exhausted, Kozyrev stressed.

He said this was indicated by the last trip to Sarajevo
of Carl Bildt, the European Union's coordinator of the
negotiation process.

Russian and American mediators are pursuing the same
policy in the conflict region.

Kozyrev said he thought it is time to pool their efforts
and set up “a group of three" which might use a single
mandate of the five-nation Contact Group.

Asked about the role of Belgrade in the settlement of
the Bosnia conflict, Kozyrev said the sanctions against
former Yugoslavia need be relaxed soon. This would be
an efficient lever in the settlement process.

Defence Miriister Pavel Grachev told the conference that
the assessment of U.N.’s treatment of all parties in the
conflict must be objective. He said Croats and Moslems
were largely responsible for conflict escalation. Grachev
said there is the need for a clearer definition of the status
of "safe zone" and for their complete demilitarisation.
He said the use of these zones for regrouping of forces
or as a Starting ground for combat must be prevented.

Grachev hailed the decision of the London meeting to
keep U.N. peacekeepers in Bosnia and Hercegovina and
expressed a readiness to send an additional contingent
of Russian peacekeepers to the region.

INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

Grachev said the peacekeepers’ mandate should be
broadened to make peace policing more efficient. He
said there is the need for a standing consultative body,
a kind of military headquarters with participation of all
states which send peacekeepers to former Yugoslavia.

The Russian defence minister said air strikes and the
use of the Rapid Reaction Force in combat would be
countermroductive. According to Grachev, the warring
parties are too close to each other, and this does not
provide a sufficient radius of safety for the air strikes.
On the other hand, military clashes with the Serbs on
the ground would be “military insanity" which would
lead to new victims, Grachev said.

Kozyrev and Grachev recalled the recent initiative of
President Boris Yeltsin who proposed a summit of G7
leaders for discussion of political solutions to the Bosnia
conflict.

Regrets Remaining ‘Differences’

LD2207170595 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service
in Russian 1623 GMT 22 Jul 95

[By ITAR-TASS diplomatic correspondent Yuriy Ko-
zlov]

{[FBIS Tratislated Text] Moscow, 22 Jul (ITAR-TASS)
— Attempts to turn the London meeting on Bosnia
into some kind of “conference for declaring aerial war"
in Yugoslavia and to "bind" Russia into a scenario
based on force were unsuccessful. Journalists from
Russia were told this today by Russian Foreign Minister
Andrey Kozyrev just befcre he flew out of the British
capital. In his view, the Russian delegation managed to
turn the debate in the direction of peace and point it
toward the discussion of political approaches.

Even so, the Russian foreign minister admitted, it did
not prove possible to draw up a joint final document.
Differences remain, he added, and, regrettably, some
States are interpreting the outcome of the conference as
a mandate for unilateral actions and for going over to
strong-arm methods. The minister stressed that Russia
will continue to hold out for the political option and to
advocate a just and balanced solution to the Yugoslav
crisis.

This opportunity, Andrey Kozyrev said, will be pro-
vided by negotiations to find a political solution. Russia,
the other members of the Contact Group, and also the
European Union should pool efforts to hold such ne-
gotiations. "However," he said, “right now our Western
partners, unfortunately, are artificially reining back the
political process.”

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

2 INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

The Russian minister pointed to Friday’s session of the
Contact Group, at which Carl Bildt, EU negotiations co-
ordinator and former Swedish prime minister, presented
a peace plan close to being endorsed. Despite this, some
countries thought it early to conclude a political accord.
As a result, the Contact Group, like the London meeting
on Bosnia, was unable to agree.

Yet Kozyrev still thinks the talks have been extremely
useful, because they made it possible to compare
various approaches and to draw on the military expertise
of many countries’ defense ministers to prove the
senselessness of trying to resolve the conflict by force.
It remains to be hoped that the “seed grain" sown in
London will grow str-ng and make it possible to avoid
confrontation and help good sense to prevail.

To Meet Serbian President in Belgrade

LD2407110495 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service
in Russian 1021 GMT 24 Jul 95

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Ivan Ivanov}

([FBIS Translated Text] Belgrade, 24 Jul (ITAR-TASS)
— The latest developments in Bosnia-Herzegovina are
to be discussed by Serbian President Slobodan Milose-
vic and Russian Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev, who
arrives in Belgrade this evening. The ITAR-TASS cor-
respondent has learned this from the Russian Embassy
in Yugoslavia.

At the talks, the head of Russian diplomacy is expected
to advise Milosevic of the results of last Friday's [21
July] London meeting of foreign and defense ministers
from the member states of the “Contact Group" on
Bosnia and the 11 countries whose military contingents
are involved in peacekeeping operations in Bosnia.

Russian Ambassador to NATO Vitaliy Churkin; Alek-
sandr Zotov, the Russian president's special represen-
tative for the affairs in former Yugoslavia; and high-
ranking representatives of the Russian Army general
staff are to arrive in Belgrade together with Kozyrev.

Kozyrev intends to return to Moscow on Tuesday
morning [25 July].

Baltic Leaders To Meet in Tallinn in Sep

WS2407094295 Tallinn ETA in English
1653 GMT 23 Jul 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Presidents of the Baltic coun-
tries are going to meet in the first week of September
in Tallinn, president’s councillor Kaja Tael told ETA.

The meeting of Estonian President Lennart Meri, and his
Latvian counterpart Guntis Ulmanis and Lithuanian col-
league Algirdas Brazauskas was discussed in Tallinn on
Friday by councillor of the Lithuanian President Justas-
Vincas Paleckis and Estonian government officials.

The last meeting of the Presidents in Jurmala, Latvia,
was the most effective and open on the estimation of
Paleckis. Free trade agreement, border-crossing, migra-
tion, joint policies on refugees from Asia and other parts
of the world, international problems were discussed in
Jurmala.

The same topics would be discussed at the Tallinn
meeting, Paleckis said.

Baltic Ministers Explain Environmental
Cooperation

WS2407094595 Tallinn ETA in English
1716 GMT 21 Jul 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] Estonian Environment Minis-
ter Villu Reiljan, and his Latvian and Lithuanian coun-
terparts Indulis Emsis environmental cooperation be-
tween the three Baltic countries on Friday in Tallinn.
[sentence as received]

Reiljan said it was a frame agreement establishing
environmental cooperation between the Baltic countries,
in the future to be coordinated by Baltic Council of
Ministers.

The main clauses of the agreement deal with economical
use of natural and mineral resources, protection of the
sea, reduction of environmental waste crossing borders
of the countries, coordination of the problems of refuse
economy, cooperation in environment-safe technology
and exchange of experiences in juridical matters. The
agreement practically covers all matters of environment
and nature protection, opening a way for future coopera-
tion in the framework of the Baltic Council of Ministers.
The agreement does not concentrate on mutual control
of the environment but information exchange, Reiljan
said.

Lithuanian Environment Minister Bronius Badauskas
considered that information exchange between the
Baltic countries very important. He said a joint moni-
toring system should be established in order to discover
pollution in time. Badauskas said environmental norms

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

exist in all three countries but they just need harmoni-
sation.

Commenting on information of the press on polluted
Daugava River, Latvian Environment Minister Emsis
said that as long as the inhabitants of Riga are not in
danger, other countries do not have to fear, as Riga gets
its drinking water from the river. Emsis assured new and
modern water purification devices have been installed
for purification of Daugava River and another 100
million U.S. dollars is expected from the World Bank
in support of the project next year. Similar investment
project is in preparation for the town of Daugavpils with
a 60 million U.S. dollar loan from World Bank.

All three ministers said their countries suffere¢c from
pollution and damage caused by the Russian troups, and
a joint Baltic politics was necessary in that field.

Reiljan said Russian troops left after them in Estonia
85.000 hectares of polluted ground, forming 5 per
cent of the Estonian territory. Emsis claimed a similar
situation in Latvia with 100.000 hectares of damaged
nature. Surface waters have been polluted in many
places and near Russian military airports water taken
from the wells burns like gasoline. The biggest Latvian
problem is Liepaja Port. "We don’t have sufficient
finances nor good projects for cleaning the water,"
Emsis said.

ITAR-TASS Carries Press Review for 22 Jul

LD2207060995 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0519 GMT 22 Jul 95

["Press Review" — ITAR-TASS headline]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow July 22 (ITAR-
TASS) — Newspapers focus their attention today on
the talks on the settlement of the Chechnya conflict,
which have been keeping public opinion of the country
in a State of tension for about a month, ROSSIYSKIYE
VESTI writes. Commenting on the developments at the
talks in Groznyy, the newspaper, which is close to the
presidential circles, stresses that "the wording of the def-
inition of Chechnya’s status became a stumbling block
on the way to peace." Although the sides agreed to
impose a moratorium on combat operations, provoca-
tions, night shootings and the death of people show that
the war in Chechnya is still going on, ROSSIYSKIYE
VESTI correspondent Aleksandr Ryabushev writes '
had the impression all the time that there are some forces
both it Moscow and in Groznyy, which do not want
bloodshed to be stopped, he stresses.

KRASNAYA ZVEZDA writes that Dudayev’s gunmen,
who openly ignore the declared armistice, have be-
come more active over the past several days, while

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI comes to the conclusion that
"the procrastination of the talks is advantageous for Du-
dayev and his men. They have already managed to re-
deploy their units and reinforce the army." According to
the newspaper, Dudayev has now at his disposal about
10,000 men.

Sergey Chugayev comments in IZVESTIYA on the
results of the “parliamentary year." His commentary
is entitled “Duma of Transitional Period Fulfilled Its
Mission." He believes that the beginning of the recess
at the lower chamber of the Russian parliament actually
means the end of the work of the fifth Duma. "The
Duma turned out to be no better and no worse than the
Government or the President’s administration. Its level
is absolutely on a par with theirs. The same as in the
executive structures, no bright personalities emerged in
the Duma,” the newspaper writes.

ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA, the newspaper of the Gov-
ernment, tries to prove that "Chernomyrdin’s electoral
bloc ‘Russia, Our Home’ and the Government are not
the same thing.” Leonard Vid, head of the executive
committee of the movement, said in an interview with
the newspaper that "we are acting on our own, indepen-
dently from the Government. Moreover, I would de-
scribe our attitude to it as critical solidarity. Our plat-
form is far broader than the economic programme of
the Cabinet of Ministers. We have our own opinion on
political, economic and social matters.”

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA frontpages an article
under the intriguing headline: "Can Shamil Basayev
Steal Atomic Bomb?" And really, can terrorists of all
kinds get hold of nuclear weapons, and how real is the
danger of a nuclear apocalypse in this country? Viktor
Roshchin, deputy head of the Main Department for the
Protection of Information, Nuclear Materials and Facil-
ities of the Ministry of Atomic Energy, answers these
questions in his interview. "It is absolutely impossible,"
he says. "Russia has a highly effective system of guard-
ing and protecting nuclear facilities, munitions and ma-
terials."

SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA responded to a report of the
Ministry of Railways about another growth of the prices
of railway transportation with a bitter caricature. ") ow
it is less expensive for a family to go to the Canary
Islands that to go to their own plot of land outside the
City,” says the inscription under the caricature.

RABOCHAYA TRIBUNA again writes about the prob-
lem of alcoholism in this country. Who become drunk-
ards in Russia and for what reasons? These questions
are answered in the newspaper by Anatoliy Shevchenko,
chief specialist on narcotics at the Ministry of Public
Health. He writes that drunkeness has swept the whole
country. Neither moral standards, nor religious norms
are working. People fall ill with alcoholism 27.7 per
cent more often than before.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

‘Complete’ Understanding With U.S. on Arms to
Iran

LD2207134795 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1310 GMT 22 Jul 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, July 22 (INTER-
FAX) — The recent sitting of Chernomyrdin-Gore com-
mission in Moscow has found solution to the issue
on Russia’s fulfillment of commitments of the former
USSR providing for the supply “f military equipment
and spare parts to Iran, aide of th. Russian prime min-
ister on international affairs Mikhail Tarasov declared
in an interview with INTERFAX.

However, he preferred not to reveal the essence of the
Russian-American agreement. "However, I can say that
we have found a complete mutual understanding on this
issue," Tarasov underlined.

Speaking about supply of light water reactors to Iran,
Tarasov expressed the opinion that Moscow should
fulfill its obligations on this contract not only because
it will bring a substantial material benefit to the country
but because "this is the issue of the country’s prestige.”
"As a matter of fact, in this case, as well as in the case
with spare parts we solve the issue whether Russia is a
reliable partner,” Tarasov underlined.

He noted that the recent sitting of Chernomyrdin-Gore
commission in Moscow also focused attention on this
subject.

"The Americans oppose any contacts with Iran. They
have introduced embargo on the trade with this country
for the American companies and tried to force Amer-
ica’s European allies to do the same. They have con-
ducted work with Russia as well, focusing attention on
the problem of nuclear non-proliferation,” Tarasov said.

He underlined that Iran is Russia’s closest neighbor. “It
is much closer to us than the United States. That’s why
Moscow itself is interested that nuclear nonproliferation
is observed in its all aspects," Tarasov said.

He also reported that there is still no solution to the
issue on reactors’ supply to Iran. "However, positions
of the countries have substantially neared,” he said.

In his words, the Russian prime minister and the US vice
president commissioned experts of two countries to find
a mutually acceptable variant. "Chernomyrdin and Gore
believe that it is necessary to find such a wording which
will satisfy both parties," Tarasov said.

He pointed out that "probably, it will be a list of the
equipment which Russia will supply to Iran and which
will not arouse and objections on the part of the United
States." "We must near our positions in appropriate
aspects and they - in some other aspects," Tarasov said.

Moscow Radio: Ties With Tehran ‘Remarkably
Improved’

LD2307103895 Tehran IRNA in English

1004 GMT 23 Jul 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] Tehran, July 23, IRNA —
Tehran-Moscow relations have remarkably improved
specially after the conclusion of the agreement for the
completion of Bushehr atomic power plant between the
two countries, Radio Moscow reported Saturday.

It added that the U.S. has repeatedy made efforts to
convince Moscow to withdraw the agreement but each
time Moscow has rejected the opposition.

It further quoted deputy Russian energy minister as
saying that the Russian Government is determined to
promote its relations with the Islamic Republic of Iran.

The official noted that Tehran is also willing to expand
bilateral ties with Moscow and to this end has proposed
for exploration and exploitation of oil within its territory
as well as in other regional states through establishment
of joint ventures, the report added.

It underlined that many Westerners believe that the U.S.
President Bill Clinton has magnified issues about Iran
under such false pretexts as Tehran attempting to get
access to nuclear weapons.

Experts from the International Atomic Energy Agency
(IAEA) have several times inspected nuclear installa-
tions in the Islamic Republic and have underlined Iran’s
peace-seeking purposes.

The radio said that the American Conoco company
terminated its oil contract with the Islamic Republic
under the U.S. pressure but the French company Total,
replacing Conoco, signed an agreement with Iran last
week.

However, in a harsh reaction towards the deal, Wash-
ington sent a message to the French Government calling
on Paris to cancel its cooperation with Iran, it said.

Karasin Cited on Russian Stance on ABM Treaty

MK2207093695 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
22 Jul 95 p2

[Report by Leonid Velekhov: "United States Charges
Russia With Refusal To ‘Clarify’ ABM Treaty. Russian
Federation Foreign Ministry: Work on ‘Clarification’ Is
Taking Its Normal Course"]

(FBIS Translated Text] The WASHINGTON TIMES
has reported that Russia turned down, in a secret diplo-
matic note, the United States’ proposal to “clarify” and
“state more precisely" some of the points in the 1992

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

ABM Treaty. In the United States’ view, the treaty limit-
ing the deployment of strategic missile defenses needed
some correction, in Darticular, a statement in more accu-
rate terms of the differences between tactical and strate-
gic systems, and the United States has more than once
tried to "move forward" the talks. The United States
also proposed letting both countries independently set
the upper limit on the speed of their missile intercep-
tors. The latest proposals were passed on 22 June to the
Russian side, which turned them down 9 July. In Rus-
sia’s view, the WASHINGTON TIMES writes, citing
an unnamed Clinton Administration spokesman, these
amendments threaten to bring bring about "excessively
varied interpretations” of the treaty as a whole and, con-
sequently, its actual invalidation.

An official Russian Federation foreign ministry
spokesman, Grigoriy Karasin, from whom your
SEGODNYA correspondent requested clarification of
the Russian stance, stated that Russia sticks to the
understandings that were reached during the May
meeting in Moscow between Presidents Yeltsin and
Clinton. In a joint statement the presidents affirmed
the main provisions of the ABM Treaty, which they
considered to be a cornerstone of world stability, and
voiced the intention to "clarify" questions related to the
permitted activities involving nonstrategic ABM assets
— on the condition, the Russian Foreign Ministry Press
Department stressed, of unswerving adherence to the
treaty. As Mr. Karasin put it, an intensive harmonizing
of viewpoints is going on between the two countries
in line with these understandings, and there had been
no need to get this “overly punctilious [shchepetilnyy]
question" into an American newspaper; all the more
so, the Russian Federation Foreign Ministry spokesman
added, now that the START II Treaty — implementa-
tion of which is coupled with the immutability of the
ABM Treaty — is up for ratification both in the Duma
and the U.S. Congress.

U.S. Aid in Neutralizing ICBM Fuel Welcomed

MM2107132095 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
in Russian 20 Jul 95 p 3

[Article by Sergey Ptichkin: "Asymmetrical Poison. It
Was Developed in Russia But the Americans Know
How To Get Rid of It"}

(FBIS Translated Text] Through historical circum-
stances, the USSR and the United States went their
separate ways in the development of strategic military
missile technology. We relied on liquid fuel, they opted
for solid fuel.

At the dawn of the missile age, the country’s chemists
developed a unique fuel additive — asymmetrical

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

dimethylhydrazine, better known as heptyl. It was
thanks to this that the world’s most powerful ICBMs
were designed and commissioned. Heptyl was found
to have many remarkable characteristics and only one
defect — it was terribly toxic. A minute concentration
of it in the air or water would inflict an agonizing death
on any living being.

It is hard to say how many of our fellow countrymen
paid with their lives for the sake of nuclear missile parity
between the USSR and the United States. Everything to
do with this liquid rocket fuel was classified top secret.

Eventually we achieved parity and the time came
to decide what to do with the surplus product of
that parity, namely, obsolete missiles which no longer
guarantee world stability but, on the contrary, threaten
an unintentional ecological catastrophe on a global
scale. The well known treaties on the reduction of
Strategic offensive weapons were signed and ratified
and timetables were drawn up for the standing down
and recycling of the antiquated leviathans of the missile
world.

Then something happened which was supposed to
happen but which no one had time to consider amid
the euphoria of the “complete and general" disarmament
inspired by the new thinking. Or rather did not want to
consider.

It was no problem for the Americans to “pulverize” their
old ICBMs strictly in accordance with the START-I
timetable and without causing any special harm to the
country’s environment. But what about Russia, the legal
heir to all the paradoxes of the USSR?

It transpired that no one knew what we were supposed
to do with the 30,000 tonnes of appallingly toxic heptyl
that was due for destruction. What a surprise! For
4u years it was produced and stockpiled, but no one
gave any thought to ways of disposing of it safely.
Who would have thought that world nuclear missile
war would not happen, that perestroyka would begin
instead, and that hundreds of missiles would have to
be destroyed not by being launched at the United
States bu: on the country’s own territory? Certainly
none of the people who might or ought to have
considered that possibility, because the vast majority
of the USSR’s population did not suspect that the top-
secret asymmetrical dimethylhydrazine even existed, of
course.

The Russian military were powerless and were unable
to keep to the START-I timetable for the withdrawal
of ICBMs. The humane Americans came to their assis-
tance. Within the framework of the Nunn-Lugar pro-
gram the Pentagon (!) set aside around $30 million

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

for the development of techniques for the safe recy-
cling of Russian hepty! and for the design and construc-
tion of special-purpose self-contained environmentally
clean hydrogenation units. The American corporation
Thiokol, which knew nothing about heptyl except that
it existed but had never had any serious dealings with
it, nevertheless won the tender in the Unticd States and
became the main subcontractor in the project to scrap
the Russian ICBM liquid fuel.

All the formal aspects seemed settled and the presen-
tation of a useful and, it must be acknowledged, vi-
tally important project for Russia took place at the
State Committee for the Defense Sectors of Industry
in Moscow.

During the presentation the commercial director of the
industrial company Allied Signal, which is going to de-
velop the heptyl destruction units for us, stated that
his firm has supplied over 600 catalytic hydrogenation
units to various countries. These units, in the Ameri-
can’s words, are absolutely safe, highly economical, and
have remarkable potential. Some of them are even be-
ing used to produce peanut oil. Mr. Defeo, representing
Allied Signal, said that the U.S. side sees no fundamen-
tal problems in the processing of heptyl and obtaining a
great many useful products from it, ranging from chem-
ical fertilizers to perfume and even valuable medical
preparations.

It is said that in our country they once learned how to
make black caviar out of black oil, so the U.S. unit with
its capabilities is a godsend. Perhaps we won't learn how
to turn heptyl into high-quality fish products but we will
surely learn how to produce butter!

The first unit for the industrial conversion of toxic
missile fuel into environmentally friendly products is
due to be built at the start of next year, and, furthermore,
not in the back of beyond but close to Sergiyev Posad,
near Moscow. When asked "Why there precisely?" a
senior official from the State Committee for the Defense
Sectors of Industry exclaimed in amazement: "Where
else?”

In the opinion of the Russian organizers of this project,
Sergiyev Posad is already full of environmentally haz-
ardous enterprises and there is already a nuclear waste
disposal site in operation there so that the heptyl in-
stallation will not harm the atmosphere locally. On the
grounds that it cannot be made any worse than it al-
ready is, presumably? If the hydrogenation unit does
not explode in the capital of the Orthodox faith near the
Russian capital itself, the next units will be built near
Krasnoyarsk in Siberia and near Zlatoust in the South
Urals.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

Furthermore, the fears of the environmentalists stem
from a lack of hard facts. And the fact is that the firm
guarantees that its brainchild is completely harmless and
safe. Admittedly, we have been given many guarantees
that "it" would never go wrong and yet it has blown up
in the past....

Nevertheless, I should like to hope that nothing will
interfere with the implementation of the Russian-U.S.
project to dispose of the Russian liquid ICBM fuel
(the “program for the joint elimination of the threat,"
as this enterprise calls it in the United States). The
Americans will keep their promises, the Russian side
will keep its side of the bargain, and it will all benefit the
planet Earth. After all, the thousands of tonnes of heptyl
stockpiled in various parts of the former USSR pose an
extremely grave threat not only to the inhabitants of our
country but the whole world too....

So we should thank the U.S. Senators Sam Nuna and
Richard Lugar for their "party" support for the project,
thank the Pentagon for the millions allocated from
its budget, thank the Thiokol Corporation for bravely
stepping out into the unknown in the fight against hepty]l,
and keep our fingers crossed that it wins this struggle,
breaks it down into tiny molecules, and converts them
into something useful. Thanks to all of them!

However, I have one question of a purely ethical nature
that might not have been raised but....

At the presentation of the project in question here, the
U.S. military department was represented by a modest
major by the name of Robert Shultz while the Russian
side was represented by a whole phalanx of major-
generals and lieutenant-generals, both those serving
within the confines of the "Arbat military district"
{Russian Defense Ministry] and those currently working
as presidents of various joint-stock companies and joint
ventures.

Our "military leaders" thrust our their chests and enthu-
siastically announced that the recycling of the heptyl is
a priority national program, that this matter is even more
important today than the recycling of nuclear waste, and
that they, the “military chiefs," have the most extensive
rights and powers.

It’s interesting. Heptyl has never been a problem for the
United States and isn’t one today, but the Americaus
have found the resources and means to resolve it.
Russia, which has faced this problem for several decades
now, has neither the money nor acceptable means
of resolving this problem. What have the generals’
impassioned words about environmental priorities got
to do with it? Have they seen the light?

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

There is no reason to doubt that the country’s scien-
tists, who managed to develop asymmetrical dimethyl-
hydrazine, could have found a way to dispose of it safely
long ago and would have done so for somewhat less than
$30 million, probably. But our "Pentagon" did not set
them such a task then, and, when the task was finally
set, it turned out that there was no money available for
this work.

The military were given only 50 percent of the amount
they requested but in reality received only 10 percent
of the estimated sum. It has perhaps become customary
to kick a government which does not does not give out
money, and all these things could probably have been
considered in the days when there was money for this
work and the time to do it.

Today, however, all that the Russian major generals can
do is gaze admiringly into the eyes of the American ma-
jors and express endless gratitude for the understanding
of Russia’s internal problems, for the material support
for the "elimination of the threat,” and for the fact that
such a remarkable and selfless country as the United
States exists.

Talks With U.S. on Exchanging Satellite Data Cited

MM2407105095 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
22 Jul 95 p3

[Report by Leonid Mlechin: "Russian Spy Satellites To
Save United States From Earthquakes and Greenhouse
Effect"]

(FBIS Translated Text] Russia and the United States are
Starting talks on exchanging spy satellite information.
That is — Russian information on U.S. territory and
U.S. information on our territory.

It transpires that Russian weather satellites have been
collecting information on cloud cover over U.S. territory
since 1965. The Americans do not have such informa-
tion. They have probably been concerned about cloud
cover over our vast country. Clouds were of interest to
Moscow and Washington for the same reason — they
might have prevented a nuclear missile strike. Now an
analysis of the information collected by our satellites
will help us to understand whether global warming re-
ally has been taking place.

Other satellites, which work exclusively for the military,
could, in the Americans’ opinion, help predict natural
disasters such as earthquakes and volcanic eruptions.

In principle approximately two-thirds of all satellites are
purely military. The general reduction in armed forces
has, as it were, freed up the satellites’ intelligence-
gathering capacity.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

As far as laymen are concerned, spy satellites photo-
graph the territory of a potential adversary, monitor its
military installations, and tap into telephone and radio
conversations. In actual fact, military satellites also pro-
vide navigational, meteorological, and geodetic infor-
mation.

Many Satellites serve the navy — above all, submarines.
Reports on the "submarine weather" allow submarines to
hide in those parts of the ocean where they are hardest to
detect. Satellites capable of measuring wave heights and
sea surface conditions in general indicate to submarines
those areas where big waves will help to mask their
engine noise and propeller wakes.

At the same time, satellites help to pinpoint potential
enemy submarines. They scan the oceans with the help
of over-the-horizon radars and lasers that are able to
penetrate up to 100 meters underwater. They also study
magnetic anomalies caused by submarines’ metal hulls,
and changes in the water temperature, which increases
after a submarine has passed by....

Navigation satellites help submerged submarines to
precisely target their nuclear-tipped ballistic missiles.

Communications satellites help submarines to precisely
determine their location. And they need to know their
precise location primarily in order to launch their
ballistic missiles.

There are currently one and at most two Russian
nuclear missile carriers on combat standby duty on the
high seas, according to certain information. The spy
satellites launched into space to track the large Soviet-
era submarine fleet clearly have little to do — which
creates favorable prospects for a mutually beneficial
exchange of information with the Americans.

Recent Fall in Trade With PPC Not Undermining
Ties

LD2307121295 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1202 GMT 23 Jul 95

[By Pavel Spirin)

(FBIS Transcribed Text) Beijing, July 23 (ITAR-TASS)
— The fall in the trade turnover between Russia and
China, when its volume dropped by 33.8 per cent in
1994, did not undermine the Chinese’ strong faith in
the most bright future of trade ties with the Russian
Federation, Deputy head of the Europe Department
of China’s Ministry for Foreign trade and Foreign
economic cooperation Wang Junwen told ITAR-TASS
today that the downward trend in export and import
levels between the two countries has almost come to a
halt in this year. [sentence as received]

China has lifted goods worth 555 million U.S. dollars
from Russia from January to May 1995, a drop of 1.8
per cent in comparison with the identical period of 1994.
At the same time, Russia has shipped to its neighbour
goods worth 1.28 million dollars, a growth of 1.5 per
cent.

The high-ranking Chinese diplomat noted that in Bei-
jing the last year’s disruption is being regarded as a
temporary phenomenon, which accompanies structural
reorganization of the entire foreign trade mechanism.
According to him, the trade between Russia and China
is passing through a transition period, which might end
by the end of the current year, when Russian President
Boris Yeltsin’s visit to China is awaited.

At present Moscow, as well as Beijing both are trying
to avoid barter system and switching over to make pay-
ments in freely convertible foreign currency. According
to the Ministry figures, it accounts for 70 to 80 per cent
of all contracts. Besides this, the governments of both
countries are preparing a legislative base designed to
regulate almost the whole spectrum of until now spon-
taneously being formed trade relations, involving finan-
cial issues, arbitrations, insurance and the provision of
transportation service. A trade strategy for the future is
also being worked out, in which a key role is being as-
signed to large and medium enterprises and commodity
producers.

Churkin on Relations With NATO, Balkans

MK2207101095 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
22 Jul 95 p4

{Interview with Vitaliy Churkin, ambassador to Bel-
gium, by Yelena Visens: “Russia’s Relations With
NATO Have an Evolutionary Character, Believes Vi-
taliy Churkin, Russian Federation ambassador to Bel-
gium;” in Brussels, date not given]

(FBIS Translated Excerpt] [passage omitted] [Visens]
What has changed since last December, when Russian
Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev refused to sign the
Partnership for Peace? What made it possible to join it
in May?

(Churkin] Intensive discussions have taken place since
December between us and NATO members, mainly the
Americans, and also within NATO itself, and, very
importantly, also at home, in Russia. After all, the
formulation of national policy on such a major issue
as European and world security is impossible without
an attempt to achieve a nationwide consensus. As a
result, the 24 May Russian Security Council decision
emerged, which was made public by Minister Kozyrev
at a meeting with his NATO colleagues a week later.
In essence it offers the following alternatives: either the
enlargement of the alliance, with the inevitable negative
consequences, or full-scale cooperation with Russia,
which has tremendous possibilities and prospects. After
accepting the program, we have begun to address the
second alternative.

[Visens] But formally nothing has changed?

[Churkin] This is not quite so. If one compares the
communique of NATO foreign ministers of 1 December
and of 30 May, one can see that they are different.
NATO has to a substantial extent resumed its emphasis
on the independent importance of the partnership. The
accent on relations with Russia has been strengthened.
Therefore the political framework of the discussion was
not the same as in December but in effect has been
transformed substantially, now corresponding more to
our perceptions than was the case on 1 December of
last year.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

[Visens] On the other hand, NATO member states are
not going to abandon their plans to expand to the East,
and sooner or later this issue will reemerge.

(Churkin] It continues to exist. For some East European
countries accession to NATO is a central political task,
on which they are working intensively. But I should not
be a fatalist here.

I think that many countries, especially those which are
clearly not among the first favorites for admission to
NATO, will be increasingly considering other, "non-
expansionist" security models. At a recent North At-
lantic Cooperation Council meeting, for example, a pro-
posal was made to discuss the consequences of nonpro-
liferation. Indeed, what will in fact happen?

[Visens}] As far as I know, there is an opinion that
NATO enlargement could complicate the situation in
Europe. Let us recall, for instance, the smoldering con-
flict between Slovakia and Hungary over the Hungarian
ethnic minority in Slovakia.

(Churkin}] That’s the point. It is one of the possible
consequences of NATO expansion. There will aisu be a
reaction to it here in some form or another. If one takes
a two- or three-year perspective, the period "optimists"
cite as the time when NATO will begin to expand,
Clearly it will be impossible to "talk" Russia into looking
at this “indifferently.” Their actions will have to be in
some way adapted to Russia’s reaction. I think that it is
realistic to bring the situation to such a state that even
if the issue of NATO expansion is not finally taken off
the agenda, it will be possible to reduce its intensity,
and no decisions will be made that will inevitably lead
to negative consequences.

Some NATO members tell us — and they do it with
such concern: You need to think more about economic
reform, create a legal system; do not think so much
about security matters. You know, such a friendly
approach. We tell them: Well, you don’t allow us to:
Was it us who began this talk of expansion? We wanted
to engage in our difficult transformations quietly and
peacefully, but then you suddenly advanced this idea
of NATO expansion to the east. Moreover, they are
talking about some vacuum. But what security vacuum
can they talk about when, for instance, Poland is an
OSCE member, a party to the CFE Treaty, a member
of the Council of North Atlantic Cooperation and the
Partnership for Peace, an associate member of the
European Union, and an associate partner of the Western
Economic Union. This is not a vacuum but a very
saturated space. We are within a sufficiently balanced
structure and the main task is not to disturb this balance.

[Visens] Are there any alternatives to NATO expansion?

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

[Churkin] An alternative to expansion is the normal
consistent logic of developing all-European cooperation,
i.e., a combination of all things, including the OSCE.
We, for instance, have advanced the idea of creating a
Council of North Atlantic Partnership and Cooperation,
that is, strengthening what is now called the North
Atlantic Cooperation Council, and the Partnership for
Peace program, their merging, which will lead to
the creation of a more serious formation than the
now divided North Atlantic Cooperation Council and
Partnership for Peace. There is very much to talk about
here.

[Visens] What is the reason the Western European
Union [WEU] has so invigorated its activity in the
recent period?

{Churkin] Western Europe is preparing for the 1996
inter-government conference and the WEU is eager
to consolidate its place in the system of all-European
institutions. WEU members want to preserve their
Originality and identity and to this end it is necessary to
show themselves, which in fact they have been doing,
and, in my view, not without success. This organization
should not be forgotten. Contacts with the WEU are not
without interest. The WEU, for example, is developing
concepts for responding to threats to European security,
and our experts could give their weighty word here.
There are also some practical avenues for cooperation,
say, the sale of our photos to the WEU satellite
communication center.

[Visens] How would you describe the current situation
in Bosnia?

{Churkin] Unfortunately, so far everything is moving
toward an abyss. And it is hard to tell how it will end.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 11

[Visens] Today there has been increasing talk to the
effect that the UN peacekeeping mission and NATO
activity in the region have failed.

[Churkin] Everybody has found himself in a very
difficult situation. We are all in it; we walk around them
in circles and cannot do anything. And if we pull out of
there, a bloody drama will begin. Whichever way one
looks at it, the situation is dreadful: It is impossible to
leave, but neither is possible to stay. It is a stalemate. It
would be good, of course, if the talks resumed, but so
far this is not happening. Each side has its own — so far
incompatible — logic, while the military option, which
is being played out now, will not lead to any good. The
distinguishing feature is that a military victory by one
side in Bosnia and Herzegovina is possible in principle.

[Visens] How do you feel about the change of cochair-
men at the EU international conference on the former
Yugoslavia?

{Churkin] Karl Bildt makes a good impression. He is a
vigorous man; he has quickly gotten to the heart of the
matter and will work hard. But there is one danger here:
He can be used, with equal ease, both in the interest of
a settlement and for moving away from the settlement.
Such a person can achieve something but, on the other
hand, his vigorous activity can be used to cover the
absence of a political line. In other words, this option is
possible: Against his own will, his activity will create
a smoke screen, while politically nothing will in effect
be changing. Therefore he is going to have a very hard
time indeed. We shall help him.

Yeltsin Appoints Barsukov Federal Security Chief

LD2407092095 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0851 GMT 24 Jul 95

[By Andrey Shtorkh]

([FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, July 24 (ITAR-
TASS) — President Boris Yeltsin appointed Mikhail
Barsukov, who was responsible for the security of top
Russian officials, as director of the Federal security
service (FSB) and promoted deputy director of the
service, Viktor Zorin, to the post of first deputy director,
the presidential press service said Monday.

The post of the director of the FSB, the successor to
the former KGB, was vacant from June 30 when Sergey
Stepashin was dismissed after the tragic raid of Chechen
terrorists on the Russian town of Budennovsk.

Barsukov, 47, was heading the Main security depart-
ment of the Russian Federation and was the comman-
dant of the Moscow Kremlin since June 1992.

He graduated from the Army higher school and the
Frunze military academy.

After graduation he served in the Kremlin regiment, was
commandant of government buildings in the Kremlin,
deputy commandant of the Kremlin and then head of
the Main security department of the Russian Federation
which is responsible for guarding top Russian official.

Legal Procedures in Chechnya Hearings Explained

MK2207054895 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
20 Jul 95 p2

[Report by Aleksey Kirpichnikov: "Constitutional Court
Shuns Politics, But There Is No Getting Away From
It. Court Chairman Sheds Light on Secrets of the
Production Process”]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Vladimir Tumanov, the Russian
Constitutional Court head, expanded on certain features
of the constitutional court procedure. Examples from the
just completed hearings on the Chechnya case, naturally,
served to illustrate the basic points.

To properly fulfill its functions, the Con.titutional
Court, according to its head, has an obligation to
shun politicization. This is facilitated, in particular, by
the hearings procedure stipulated in the Law on the
Constitutional Court.

Its basic principle is a contest between the parties.
It is their responsibility to seek out the arguments,
prove them, and disprove the opponent. According to
Mr. Tumanov’s ideal of legal procedure, the judges
should in no way interfere in the dispute between the
plaintiff and the respondent, but simply follow the
proceedings and determine whether the plaintiff has
proven his claim. The case can be conducted without
appearances in court and the matter examined by written
correspondence, which is the practice in a number of
countries. Finding experts and witnesses is also the
responsibility of the parties. The Constitutional Court
should give the floor to experts only on subjects far
removed from jurisprudence, or on narrow areas of it
such as housing legislation; even greater care must be
taken in allowing witnesses, since the Constitutional
Court (as distinct from the general courts) does not have
any procedural possibilities of verifying the facts at its
disposal.

In Mr. Tumanov’s opinion, the Constitutional Court
took a step toward the ideal when it examined the
Chechnya case. In the previous hearings, after the par-
ties had presented their cases, they were questioned by
the judges, while now the defendant has been exam-
ining the plaintiff, and the plaintiff the defendant. The
parties called in vain for the judges to explain various
legal norms or obtain various pieces of evidence: The
court is under no obligation to do anyone else’s work for
them. It became logical that the number of experts and
specialists was comparatively small: The Constitutional

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

Court refused to hear a number of renowned lawyers,
whose opinion was already known to the court, or wit-
nesses from the Federation Council, who had signed the
petition to the Constitutional Court and were thus not
objective. A few lawyers were admitted simply for the
sake of diplomacy (to keep a balance in the lists of
the plaintiff and the defendant) and thev said exactly
what was expected. Sergey Xovalev spoke eloquently,
although it was irrelevant to the legal aspect of the prob-
lem.

The ‘‘»nstitutional Court head dwelled on Valeriy
Zorkin’s request to prolong the hearings. Under the
law, examination of the case may be prolonged if new
circumstances come to light in the final summations
by the parties. This was not the case, however. The
Constitutional Court rejected the request "by a very
large number of votes" and, as for the complaints that
the aspects discussed earlier were vague, Mr. Zorkin
should have lodged them against himself of the previous
hearings. [sentence as published]

Mr. Tumanov stated that both parties had shown in-
sufficient skill in conducting their contest; moreover,
the plaintiff was poorly prepared to prove his case.
He rejected accusations by Federation Council member
Yelena Mizulina that there was procedural inequality,
adding that Mrs. Mizulina herself, by her conduct in
the courtroom (facial expressions and vocalizing) de-
served a far more severe punishment than the warning
she had once received.

Wien it came to describing the substance of the ruling
on the case and the procedure under which it was arrived
at, Vladimir Tumanov, naturally, was inscrutable. The
judges’ deliberations are due to start “approximately”
today, but the chairman does not know how long they
will take and what the nature of the verdict will be.
Any nonpoliticized ruling by the Constitutional Court,
however, will inevitably bear a political nature and
significance.

Chernomyrdin Response to Skokov’s Comments
Viewed

MK2207125095 Moscow ZAVTRA in Russian
No. 29, Jul 95 (Signed to press 18 Jul) p 1

[Report by V. Aleksandrov: “Chernomyrdin Versus
Skokov"}

{(FBIS Translated Text] Some time ago a meeting
was held between some mass media chiefs and
Yuriy Skokov — quite a notable event since Yuriy
Vladimirovich Skokov, leader of the Congress of
Russian Communities and the Congress of Peoples of
Russia, rarely meets the press, although he is ranked

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 13

among the most astute and well informed politicians in
the country.

Comments have appeared in a number of publications
concerning some statements by Yu.V. Skokov at that
meeting. For instance, Yuriy Vladimirovich described
Vikior Chernomyrdin as the “main mafia boss in the
country.” In 1991, as president of the Gazprom joint-
stock company, Chernomyrdin asked the Russian Gov-
ernment for (and actually received) 4 billion rubles to
keep his concern afloat. When last year Skokov asked
the premier why Gazprom, which only recently was beg-
ging for money from the state, today has $176 million
to buy shares of the Russian Vneshtorgbank, he could
not come up with any coherent explanation.

Also, Skokov recalled that while Chubays assessed
Gazprom’s assets at $298 million, Western experts cite
altogether different figures: from $250 billion to $900
billion. And the premier holds 5 percent of Gazprom’s
stock. It is not accidental that Chernomyrdin has a
private airplane ready to take him to the West at any
moment; but, Yuriy Vladimirovich believes, nobody
will escape people’s retribution; a Nuremburg trial
awaits all plunderers of Russia.

The following day Chernomyrdin received a detailed
account of this meeting with Skokov. Although the
premier was enraged, nonetheless ii took more than
two weeks to prepare a public "rebuke" to Skokov.
On one hand, the premier could not but respond; on
the other, Chernomyrdin and his inner circle knew
that, considering Skokov’s special awareness, it was
impossible to treat his statements simply as an emotional
outburst.

After agonizing reflection the response to Skokov was
in the end made up and published in several papers on
behalf of Gazprom’s public relations department.

The "response to Skokov" turned out rather strangely.
Yes, Chernomyrdin says, Gazprom did take money
from the state in 1991. But as for the $176 million, I
can’t remember. At the same time the premier’s experts
totally ignored the key point.

One of the main reasons domestic industry has found
itself on the verge of collapse is that Chernomyrdin’s
monopolist Gazprom in the "reform" years raised —
without any re: aint — the price of natural gas, at one
time a national asset. This allowed him to accumulate
tremendous free assets, including in Western banks.
While tens of thousands of enterprises have had wage
arrears for months and millions of ordinary people
have to live on a near-starvation diet, the monopolist
sponsored by the premier is thriving all the more.
The $176 million spent to buy Vneshtorbank shares is

just One element of this expansion. The Chernomyrdin-
Gazpro:n team is buying newspapers, banks, and the
most profitable enterprises on a grand scale.

Yes, Chernomyrdir says, Skokov is right that the as-
sessment of Gazprom’s aggregate assets varies between
$250 billion and $940 billion. But he remembers sud-

Chernomyrdin’s Bloc Discusses Political Stance

LD210720419, Moscow INTERFAX in English
1836 GMT 2] Jul 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, July 21 (INTER-
FAX) — The Cur Home Is Russia political bloc, led
by Prime Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin, stands for "a
powerful federal executive branch of government that is
headed by the president,” according to a political plat-
form document the organization has drafted for itself.

The draft is due to be discussed by a conference of
leaders of the bloc’s regional branches Saturday.

"The democratic choice enshrined in the Constitution of
Russia of 1993 is the choice of a constitutional feder-
ation and not of a federation based on an agreement,"
the document reads in part. As for agreements between
the central authorities and the regions, those "must be
used as an additional instrument for taking account of
the specific economic, ethnic or regional character of
such constituent parts of the Russian Federation.”

The government must also be free from “the petty
tutelage of parliament,” a body which “must bring out
laws and not engage in pointless discussions on political
issues.”

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

One of the bloc’s priority objectives is Russia’s terri-
torial integrity, which must involve "a ban on turning
administrative borders into national or economic ones."
Unilateral attempts by Russian regions to change their
status are also intolerable.

"The priority of federai legislation throughout the terzi-
tory of the country means that coercive measures must
be used against constituent parts of the Russian Fed-
eration which cause detriment to the national interests
of Russia or fail to meet their commitments under the
Constitution,” the draft says.

Shokhin Describes ‘Russia Is Our Home’ Platform

MM2107121195 Moscow TRUD in Risssian
21 Jul 95 pp 1,5

[Transcript of interview with Aleksandr Shobhia, chair-
man of the Program Commission of the Russia Is Our
Home movement, by Olga Solomonova uncer the "Po-
litical Spectrum" rubric; place and date not given: "Un-
der the Roof of ‘Home...’" — first two paragraphs are
TRUD introduction]

[FBIS Translated Text] A scientific-practical conference
of the Russia Is Our Home political movement is
opening at Moscow’s Izmaylovo movie and concert
complex tomorrow. The conference will discuss the
movement's election platform. What, then, does the
“party of government,” headed by the prime minister,
stand for apart from the desire to hold on to power?
What kind of program is it going to the elections with?

The draft program is here described by State Duma
Deputy Aleksandr Shokhin, a former government vice
premier who is today president of the Higher School of
the Economy and chairman of the Russia Is Our Home’s
Program Commission.

{Shokhin] Certainly, unlike all the other electoral blocs
od associations, Russia Is Our Home does have a spe-

» status. Given that representatives of the movement
hold key positions in the government, the following
question arises: If the program offering a “bright fu-
ture" promised in the platform cannot be realized right
now, where is the guarantee that these people will be
capable of carrying it out after the elections? Moreover,
this argument will be one of the main ones deployed in
our opponents’ election rhetoric. Hence the pretty tough
demands made on the Russia Is Our Home platform and
on the activity of its representatives in government, par-
ticularly the premier.

First and foremost, the program will differ from today’s
specific government action because it is geared to the
next four years and because the movement is a broader
formation than the cabinet or the "party of government,”

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 15

as we are called. Consequently, modifications to the
government program are inevitable — they should
reflect a certain area of compromise, the bounds of
which we will be attempting to define in our election
platform. At the same time it is understandable that
members of the government are unable to diverge
sharply from the basic tenets of the current course.

Based on these criteria, we have prepared a draft
election platform esserially made up of theses. And
we will be discussing them at the conference. It is
a question of clarifying the movement’s stances. This
may require a rethinking of the government’s current
activity in the light of plans for the future and the
broadening of Russia Is Our Home’s social base...by
attracting supporters among other political forces. But
it is essential that we clearly define our centrist course
here, delineating the acceptable bounds of political and
economic compromises.

Enhancing the role of the state, including its role in the
economy, is one of the key features of the platform. But
this is not a reversion to authoritarian government as
understood, say, by the Communists or the Democratic
Party of Russia. We are talking about effective state
control over the rules of market behavior as defined by
legislation. And here Russia Is Our Home occupies
strong-state positions, but it does so within the
context of democratic elections and the strengthening
of the institutions of the market economy.

A distinctive feature of our platform resides in the
fact that the platform cannot be held "in suspension"
until the elections. If we formulate certain fundamental
innovations in it, we will have to get down to them
ripht now, although it would be more effective and
advantageous to hold them back until the elections.
But our promises could quickly become devalued if the
premier — as the leader of the movement — is unable to
Carry out a number of the platform’s key provisions as
part of the government’s activity. And the foundations
for this must be laid today.

Ts2 draft platform is underpinned by a new economic
policy course — the switch from the liberal stage of
development toward steady economic growth based on
the accumulation of national capital. It is essential
that we try right now, without disrupting financial
stabilization, to foster the emergent shoots of economic
recovery.

These are, as a rule, export-oriented and are mainly
found in the raw-materials sectors. While developing,
they naturally experience a need for machinery and
equipment. Our task is to satisfy that need in our
domestic market by stimulating recovery in the allied
sector of machine building. Government efforts are

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

16 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

essential here, particularly in providing export credit
guarantees, increasing the investment component of
privatization, and introducing the kind of financial
instruments which will make it possible to switch from
foreign loans to the mobilization of resources in the
home market (and they are considerable — the premier
has cited the figure of $25 billion and 70 trillion rubles).

Quite a few such mechanisms have been developed, and
they could all be quickly set in train. And the state’s
task is to go ahead with them rather than wait until they
appear with time of their own accord. And economic
development is the basis for increasing the standard of
living and tackling social problems. There is no other
way.

Interview With ‘Russia Is Our Home’ Official

MM72407101995 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
Weekend Edition in Russian 22 Jul 95 p 3

{Interview with Leonard Vid, leader of the “Russia
Is Our Home" Executive Committee, by Aleksandr
Batygin; place and date not given: "Serious Work for
Serious People. V. Chernomyrdin’s Electoral Bloc and

paragraph is ROSSIVSKAYA GAZETA introduction]

(FBIS Translated Tex*] A scientific and practical con-
ference of the all-Rusz‘a “Russia Is Our Home” move-
ment is being held today. According to the founding
congress, the movement is intended to create reliable
legislative underpinnings and a responsible majority in
the State Duma and to bring "serious people accustomed
to serious work” into parliament. Prime Minister Viktor
Chernomyrdin has been elected chairman of the move-
ment. The delegates adopted rules and a political state-
ment. The keyword for the "Russia Is Our Home” bloc
is political stability, economic predictability, and active
support for the social sphere, the movement's main aims
are connected with strengthening the national economy,
legality, citizens’ security, and so forth. By edict of
the Russian Federation president, the elections of State
Duma deputies are scheduled for 17 December. Thus
the election campaign in the country has been officially
launched. The "Russia Is Our Home” movement is join-
ing in the election marathon. Leonard Vid, chairman of
the movement’s Executive Committee, tells of its im-
mediate tasks and of preparations for the elections.

[Batygin] What has happened since 12 May, when the
"Russia Is Our Home” founding congress was held?
What have you been doing during this time?

[Vid] The organizational formalities for our movement
are now being completed. It has been registered at the
Ministry of Justice and its working organ — a nine-

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

member Executive Committee — has been elected. It
held its first session 3 July.

[Batygin] The influence of some parties and movements
does not extend beyond Moscow, and often noi beyond
the Garden Ring. But with you the center of the
movement’s entire work has originally been in the
regions...

[Vid] Even before the first congress action groups were
created in 79 Federation components. Today constituent
conferences to establish independent regional organiza-
tions have already been held in 60 Federation compo-
nents. We estimate that they will have been held in the
remaining regions by the end of July.

[Batygin] Attitudes toward the pro-government bloc
among society and the press are extremely mixed. You
are openly described as the "bosses’ party.” Remember
that the founding congress was attended by many
ministers, heads of oblast administrations, leaders of
republics within Russia, and the directors of the giant
VAZ, GAZ, and KamAZ motor vehicle plants. Do you
share those assessments?

[Vid] When people in the mass media or the Duma
call us the “bosses’ party" for some reason they forget
the other aspect of the matter. The leadership of
our regional organizations, Executive Committee, and
council is swelled by people who can be described
as professionals. They include doctors and workers,
performers and bankers, entrepreneurs and engineers.
Now, after the publication of the president’s edict on
elections, we are very much hoping for individual
membership. Let me explain: Nobody has any intention
of establishing “primary cells" in enterprises and labor
collectives — in any case the law does not allow this.
If a regional department or branch of the movement
has now been created in the oblast or city, somebody
who shares our ideas can go there and apply to join the
movement. Then it is up to him to tell of his choice at
work. We have already seen for ourselves that we are
supported — and not only by the bosses. Our slogans
are simple and clear: extricating the country and the
Russian economy from crisis as swiftly as possible, and
helping those who are below the poverty line.

{Batygin] The movement’s political statement says:
"Financial stabilization is not an aim in itself."

[Vid] Correct. Our main aim is to create the regional
conditions in the country for the start of economic
growth that would make it possible to lift the country
to new heights and increase social support for the
population.

{Batygin] For many people “Russia Is Our Home"
and the government are synonymous. The opposition

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

constantly emphasizes this and criticizes you. What are
your relations with the government like, and to what
extent are you dependent on it? Do you often talk with
Chernomyrdin?

[Vid] Let me reiterate what was emphasized at the
congress: We act independently of the government, and
moreover I would describe our attitude toward it as
a state of critical solidarity. I believe our platform is
substantially broader than the economic program of
the cabinet of ministers. We have our own opinion
about political, economic, and social questions. In our
view, the potential of the liberal market model is now
exhausted and today we need to turn toward more
active state participation in economic affairs. Then we
can hope i achieve stabilization in the economy, and
therefore raise living standards, increase social support,
and resolve the unemp!oyment problem. This kind of
State participation must be clearly defined by the laws
adopted by the Duma, with deputies who know life
and its realities not merely from books and rallies but
from practical experience taking part in the lawmaking
process.

Russia’s huge expanses require a centrally managed
transport infrastructure, and the country’s defense ca-
pability is also tied up with this. The people who will
enter parliament on our election ticket are well aware
of this.

Viktor Stepanovich Chernomyrdin talks with us only

{Batygin] Which of the maiiy parties and movements

main point along the road toward our objective, we will
form a bloc both during the election campaign and in
the future parliament. Of course, we cannot go along
with those who call for extremism and civil war.

We want to hold talks with the Agrarian Party of Russia.
We think that at present the Agrarians are primarily a
lobbying party, but the party should become a political
force organizing the upsurge of agriculture. We will

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 17

scarcely be able to find common ground with the Liberal
Democratic Party of Russia and the Communist Party
of the Russian Federation. The position of the latter on
emerging from the crisis coniradicts our position. There
will be no return to the past, but the Communists base
a great deal on that assumption.

{Batygin] Leonard Borisovich, we cannot avoid the
question of the movement’s funding. Remember that
literally the day after the founding congress some of the
mass media criticized you for providing a free dinner
for delegates and guests. So it appears that you are not
poverty-stricken?

[Vid] I can explain how rich we ere by using the
following illustration. In two months of work on the
Executive Committee my colleagues and I have been
paid our wages just once. I would point out that

will have to express their comments and proposals. The

tell us a bit about yourself.

[Vid] I graduated with distinction from the Moscow
Institute of Nonferrous Metals and Gold and spent 22
years working by assignment at the Norilsk Mining and
Enriching Combine, spending half this time as deputy
general director for economics. Then I moved to the
capital, to the USSR State Planning Committee, and rose
to first deputy chairman. I have no intention of becoming
a minister or deputy — I am rather too old for that. But

FAX) — Vladimir Zhirinovskiy’s ultranationalist Lib-
eral Democratic Party of Russia [LDPR] is the country’s
best known political organization, according to the Rus-
sian Public Opinion Center (VTsIOM).

Thirty-five percent of those questioned in an opinion
poll taken by VTsIOM early this month said they knew

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

about the LDPR, the Center said in a report obtained by
INTERFAX.

The Communist Party was known to 34 percent of
the 1,600 urban residents asked to name all political
parties, movements and coalitions they knew. Thirty-
one percent had heard of the Yabloko reformist bloc,
21 percent named Yegor Gaydar’s reformist Democratic
Choice of Russia, 18 percent mentioned the conservative
Agrarian Party, and 10 percent knew the reformist
Democratic Russia.

None of the other organizations was able to get above
the 10 percent mark. Among them were the moderately
reformist political bloc Our Home Is Russia, led by
Prime Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin, parliamentary
speaker Ivan Rybkin’s Regions of Russia coalition and
the Party of Russian Unity and Concord.

New Residency Registration Rules Approved

MK2207081895 Moscow MOSKOVSKIY
KOMSOMOLETS in Russian 21 Jul 95 pl

(Unattributed report: "The Residence Permit System on
Russia’s Territory Is To Be Superseded by Residence
Registration")

[FBIS Translated Text] The Russian Government has

approved new rules for the residence registration of
Russian Federation citizens.

As MOSKOVSKIY KOMSOMOLETS was advised at
the Russian Federation presidential staff Information
Department, as of now the notion of a residence
permit [propiska] is abolished. It is being replaced by
registration. As before, the militia is still the registering
body.

New terms are being introduced: "temporary residence
registration" and “permanent residence registration."
A temporary residence is the place where a citizen
Stays temporarily: a hotel, sanitarium, vacation home,
hospital, and so forth. Permanent residence is the place
where the citizen resides permanently as the owner or
tenant of the living quarters he occupies.

All citizens are obligated to effect temporary residence
registration if they stay in another town for more than
10 days. In this event they must present to registration
officials an ID paper, an application, and a document
serving as grounds for temporary residence. Registration
is effected within three days, whereupon citizens are
issued a temporary residence registration certificate.

Procedures for permanent residence registration re-
main unchanged, except that as of now instead of the
"propiska” stamp a permanent residence registration
mark is made in the passport. Furthermore, citizens are

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

issued a certificate attesting that they live in the place
they live. Registration fees are the same as they are now
for the propiska.

New frocedures are also being introduced for the reg-
istration of clergy and refugees. Thus, monks and nuns
will be registered by monastery or convent superiors,
with the registration data are to be transferred to the
militia. Refugees are only registered in the event that
they have an ID and an appropriate certificate from the
Federal Migration Service.

The registration nrocedure itself should be worked out
by the Interiiai Affairs Ministry within the next three
months.

Military Issues
Strategic Missile Forces Chief on Inspection Trip

MM2407104695 Moscow KRASNAYA ZVEZDA
in Russian 22 Jul95 pl

{Report by Lieutenant Colonel Ilshat Baychurin: "From
the Strategic Missile Forces: Commander in Chief on
Combat Alert Duty")

{FBIS Transl ited Text] Arounu 20,000 servicemen are
on combat alert duty in the Strategic Missile Forces
[SMF] every day. Owing to lack of funding and

undermanning, missilemen are sometimes at their duty
stations for 15-18 days a month. Everyone — from
the commander in chief to a lieutenant — shares
responsibility at the SMF panels for the reliability of
the country’s nuclear missile shield.

Colonel General Igor Sergeyev, SMF commander in
chief, is currently on combat alert duty at the Central
Command Post. He has repeatedly inspected the actions
of the alert forces in tackling various tactical problems.
Incidentally, it is exactly 40 years since Igor Dmitriye-
vich began his service in the Armed Forces. And, as is
customary among the military, he celebrated this date
at his post.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 19

measures in connection wits memorable events in the
fatherland’s rrilitary history and for veterans’ affairs.

The standing committee is headed by V.S. Cher-
nomyrdin, chairman of the Russian Federation Govern-

chairman of the Russian Federation Government (com-
mittee first deputy chairman). The committee’s deputy

A.A. Bogdanov, deputy chief of the Russian Federation
Armed Forces General Staff.

to make annual provision for funding the activity of

technical support is entrusted to the Russian Fedeza-

sures in connection with the celebration of memorable
dates in the Great Patriotic War of 1941-1945 into re-
gional committees to prepare for and conduct measures
in connection with memorable events in the fatherland’s
military history and for veterans’ affairs.

Divisions in Duma Committee Noted

LD2107200595 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1836 GMT 21 Jul 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, July 21 (INTER-
FAX) — A commission appointed by the State Duma,
Russia’s lower house of parliament, to investigate into
the causes of the Chechnya crisis has been unable to
produce a report due to discord among its members, the
commission chairman said Friday.

Stanislav Govorukhin, leader of the Democratic Party
faction in the Duma, told a house session that four of
the commission’s 10 members had refused to sign a
draft report. The four represent the Democratic Choice
of Russia party, the Yabloko bloc, the Party of Russian
Unity and Concord and the Duma’s Stability group,
Govorukhin said.

He said each of the four members was now due to
present his own conclusions, after which a combined
two-volume report would be presented to the Duma
this autumn. After that the commission was likely to
be disbanded, he said.
