*Yabloko’ Leader Criticizes Tough Monetary Policy

MM1907135795 Moscow IZVESTIYA (Financial
Section) in Russian 18 Jul 95 p 4

[Article by Grigoriy Yavlinskiy: "The Government's
Onslaughts Only Stir Up Russian Inflation. Grigoriy
Yavlinskiy, Leader of the ‘Yabloko’ Party, Analyzes
the Causes of Failures in Combating Inflation and
Proposes a Program for the Country’s Recovery From
the Economic Crisis"]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Nobody is likely to dispute
the view that throughout the recent years of Russian
reforms, the reformers’ key task was and is to combat
inflation, and that monetary policy is the most important
(if not the only) instrument in this struggle. An analysis
of monetary policy from 1992-1994 makes it possible
to say that two attacks have been made on inflation.

Experience

The first attack was made in the first half of 1992 —
hot on the heels of the January price liberalization,
when a tough monetary policy aimed at reducing the
inflation rate was being pursued fairly consistently.
During that period, an increase in the ruble exchange
rate was achieved. The rate of growth of the money

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

supply was kept within the bounds of 9-14 percent per
month, that is, at a fairly low level (bearing in mind that
the real money supply, in the context of dramatically
soaring prices, was reduced fivefold, to approximately
20 percent of the December 1991 level). Consequently,
the monthly inflation rate fell from 38.3 percent in
February to 11.9 percent in May. The "downside" of the
falling inflation rate was the deep recession in industrial
production and the colossal defaults crisis. It was these
circumstances (and not populism or political weakness
on the part of the government) that determined the
retreat that began in the summer of the same year: The
government could not bring itself to continue to insist
on pursuing a tough monetary policy in the context of
these mutual defaults by enterprises (as well as the wage
debts) and the most profound industrial recession in
peacetime (18 percent, on the basis of the 1992 results).

Viktor Gerashchenko, the new leader of the Bank of
Russia, introduced reciprocal settlement of enterprises’
debts, increased the rate of growth of the money
supply two- or three-fold, and reduced hard currency
interventions on the domestic currency market, allowing
the ruble exchange rate to fall. As a result of these
measures the real money supply was doubled; the scale
of the defaults crisis was reduced considerably; the
ruble "fell" 75 percent in the second half of 1992;
and commercial banks accumulated enormous and little-
used monetary resources. The inflation rate doubled
(26.1 percent in November), but on the other hand
the fall upsurge in industrial production was vigorous
enough to alleviate the consequences of the profound
spring recession.

The second attempt to tackle inflation began in late
1992-early 1993. It differed from the previous attempt
only tactically: Whereas in the first half of 1992, the
Russian Government was trying to conquer inflation
by means of a "shock" (that is, very tough) monetary
policy, now the course set was that of a gradual,
progressive "squeeze" on the money supply and a
correspondingly smooth lowering of the inflation rate.

For nearly the whole of the first half of 1993 there
was success in ensuring a steady reduction (by approx-
imately 1 percentage point per month) in the inflation
rate, which was 18.5 percent in May. In general this
was not accompanied either by a deterioration in the in-
dustrial dynamic, or by any exacerbation of the defaults
situation. However, the further tightening of credit and
monetary policy galvanized a whole series of negative
processes that had hitherto been in a relatively stable
state.

In using exclusively monetary anti-inflation instruments,
the Russian Government was hoping that the attainment

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

of the planned inflation indicators (7 percent in Decem-
ber 1994) would boost the economic situation of its own
accord: A boom in private investments would begin im-
mediately, foreign capital would pour in, the IMF would
grant credits, and so on. Acting within this framework,
the government stubbornly continued to "squeeze" the
money supply, reducing it in the last months of 1993 to
a record low level of 14 percent of the December 1991
level (in real terms).

Judging by the dynamics of inflation, events were ap-
parently continuing to develop in the desired direction:
During the fourth quarter of 1993, the inflation rate was
reduced markedly — by almost half. But at the same
time the consequences of an "over-tough” monetary pol-
icy began to emerge. The first, immediate such conse-
quence was the rapid increase in reciprocal payment de-
faults between enterprises. The ratio of the deferred debt
to suppliers to enterprises’ monetary resources, which
had fallen to 83 percent by mid-1993, then began to in-
crease dramatically — to 133 percent on 1 October and
190 percent on 1 January 1994.

Whereas in early 1992, the main reaction to the tough
monetary policy was the growth in defaults, in late
1993 enterprises were actively reducing the volume of
production, alongside the increase in reciprocal debts.
As a consequence, the seasonal autumn revival in
production in 1993 was very sluggish (whereas in spring
there had been a reduction in the rate of the seasonal
decline compared with 1992). The result was that the
scale of the industrial recession, on the basis of the 1993
results, remained virtually at the level of the previous
year.

The arrival of 1994 saw no change in the restrictive
nature of the government’s monetary policy. A further
reduction in the average monthly rate of increase in the
M2 money supply was secured — to 8.6 percent in
the first quarter of 1994 (compared with 12.3 percent
in the fourth quarter of 1993 and 17 percent in the
third quarter). Once again, a substantial reduction in
the inflation rate was achieved — from 17.9 percent in
January to 7.4 percent in March. A virtually parallel
reduction in the rate of growth of the M2 money supply
and the inflation rate made it possible throughout that
period to maintain the size of the real money supply
at an exceptionally low level — 14 percent of the
December 1991 level.

A toughening of monetary policy and a significant
state debt for output that was purchased but not paid
for in 1994 helped to further exacerbate enterprises’
financial position. Consequently the deferred debt to
suppliers by the end of the first quarter was already three
times the enterprises’ monetary resources. The economy

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 47

couid not sustain such rigorous financial restrictions, and
responded with a colossal reduction — by almost one-
fourth — in the physical volume of industrial production
in the first quarter of 1994. In March the volume
had fallen to the level of the predicted graph (based
on 1993 trends) of industrial dynamics constructed for
1995. The deflationary shock of the first three months
of 1994 was basically a natural consequence of the
government’s attempts to keep down the inflation rate at
all costs. After this, a period of relaxation again began
in monetary policy.

This last assertion may appear incorrect: After all, the
inflation rate continued to fall gradually even after the
end of the first quarter — to a record low of 4.6 percent
in August. Nonetheless, it is true: The average monthly
rate of growth of the M2 money supply, which was 8.6
percent in the first quarter, rose to nearly 14 percent in
the second quarter; in August the rate of growth of the
M2 money supply was 12 percent.

This paradox is explained not only (and not even
chiefly) in terms of the time lag between the growth
of the money supply and the acceleration of inflation
but also by one fairly obvious fact. In spring and
summer 1994, the construction of financial "pyramids"
in the Russian economy, the period of the "soap bubble
economy,” reached its peak. The population’s money,
intended for the consumer market, was to a significant
extent diverted into the market for speculative financial
operations.

Since the reduction in the inflation rate at this stage
was not achieved by toughening monctary policy, the
real money supply in April-August 1994 increased very
rapidly and, by the end of that period, reached 20
percent of the December 1991 level.

The growth in the real money supply immediately af-
fected the dynamics vi defaults and industrial produc-
tion. Beginning in April, there was some slowing of the
growth of the relative size (compared with enterprises’
own monetary resources) of the deferred debt to suppli-
ers; the trend toward the acceleration of the recession in
industrial production was interrupted, and August even
saw the start of an "upward" movement. Thus the re-
treat in monetary policy led to a corresponding trend in
industrial dynamics.

Finally, with the end of the period of the "soap bubble
economy,” the correlation between the dynamic of the
M2 money supply and the inflation rate was restored,
and in the first quarter of 1994 inflation soared to 15-16
percent a month. The inflationary potential accumulated
during that period began to be released, and it could
not be held back by a toughening of monetary policy in
late 1994: Although the average monthly rate of growth

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

48 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

in the nominal M2 money supply in the fourth quarter
of 1994 fell to 6-7 percent, inflation continued to gain
ground and in January 1995 it peaked at 18 percent.

This was the starting position from which the Russian
economy entered the year 1995, and the year began
with yet another toughening of monetary policy —
judging by preliminary data, a very dramatic one.
The government has launched a new, third attack on
inflation.

First of all, the government declined to use Bank
of Russia credits to cover the federal budget deficit,
because of the obvious inflationary consequences of that
policy. Instead, it was decided to go over entirely to
a noninflationary mechanism for financing the budget
deficit — by borrowing free monetary resources on the
financial market by means of placing state securities.
Second, the Bank of Russia in effect stopped granting
centralized credits to commercial banks. Third, despite
the practically 100-percent receipt of income, budget
expenditure was drastically cut (by nearly 20 percent, on
the basis of the results of the first four months). Fourth,
by means of a series of rigorous administrative measures
and massive hard currency interventions the Bank of
Russia stabilized not only the situation on the currency
exchange, but also the trend toward a strengthening of
the ruble exchange rate, which also had a quite definite
anti-inflationary effect. All this made it possible to cut
the inflation rate from 18 percent in January to a level
of 7-9 percent in April-June.

Results

The failure of the first two attempts seems obvious
enough now — the dynamic of inflation since January
1992 speaks for itself. However, there may exist in our
economy a certain threshold as regards the toughening
of monetary policy, beyond which its negative side-
effects begin to outweigh the positive deflationary
effect. Is it right for the Russian Government to use
almost exclusively monetary methods for combating
inflation?

I think that even this cursory glance at the history of
combating inflation in Russia’s economy allows us to
formulate certain important conclusions for macroeco-
nomic policy.

1. In the course of the attacks on inflation that have
been launched since 1992, an empirical limit on the
toughening of credit and monetary policy has become
Clear. It is very important to note, in this connection,
that this limit occurs at an inflation rate other than
zero: Analysis shows that this lower limit is at the
level of price increases of around 8-9 percent a month.
This suggests that a certain quite specific level of

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

inflation is inherent in the institutional and industrial
structure of the Russian economy. This limit could
be called "structural-institutional background inflation"
(hereinafter, for brevity, the “background” level of
inflation). Let us note in passing that, strictly speaking,
even a monthly inflation rate approximating to the 8-9
percent level is achieved as a result of fairly specific
measures — such as, for instance, the postponement of
foreign debt payments; an increase in the domestic debt;
the significant underfinancing of science and education
(that is, future scientific and technical potential) and of
infrastructure sectors. On this basis, a level of inflation
of 8-9 percent a month, termed “background,” could
even be considered rather too low, that is to say,
extremely tough.

2. From the viewpoint of the rigorousness of credit and
monetary policy, research carried owt at the Economic
and Political Research Center showec. that today that
limit is a 13-15 percent level of the real M2 money
supply (as a percentage of December 1991). During
those three years of combating inflation, this "toughness
limit" on monetary policy fell gradually — from 20
percent at the beginning of 1992 to 13-15 percent at
the beginning of 1995. However, this is mainly due to
the reduction over that period in the volume of GDP,
as well as the increase in the dollarization of domestic
— mainly unofficial — trade turnover. This obviously
indicates the insignificance of fundamental institutional
reforms in Russia’s economy. In the medium term, we
could see this parameter as one of the controlling factors
in the adjustment of the current parameters of anti-
inflation policy.

3. Where the inflation rate is higher than the “back-
ground" level and the real money supply exceeds the
13-15 percent mark, a restrictive anti-inflation policy is
effective: It does indeed make it possible to reduce the
inflation rate without a general deterioration in indica-
tors relating to the industrial dynamic and the general
economic situation.

4. When the monthly inflation rate and the size of the
real money supply fall below the said limits, a further
toughening of monetary restrictions becomes increas-
ingly inexpedient: The effectiveness of monetary mea-
sures is reduced sharply; at the same time their neg-
ative side-effects increase substantially and the effect
of “over-toughening" of credit and monetary policy ap-
pears. Apart from the high price that must be paid for
each percentage point of additional reduction in inflation
through “over-toughening," the reduction in inflation is
itself largely a phantom. Inflation takes on depressed
characteristics: The shortage of money in the economy
is partially compensated for by an increase in defaults,
and partially by an intensification of the recession. Until

{This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
| is proaibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

wach time as the government is capable of implement-
ing an effective policy of bankruptcies and cleaning up
enterprises’ finances, a toughening of credit and mon-
etary policy is pointless. The coiled spring of inflation
is squeezed and then inevitably released again — just
as soon as the exacerbation of the »roblem of recession
and defaults once again forces a relaxation of credit and

monetary policy.

5. The government should focus its efforts not on try-
ing to reduce the inflation rate by means of “over-
toughening,” but on reducing the actual limit on "tough-
ness" — that is, the "background" level of inflation ini-
tiated by factors of a nonmonetary nature. To this end
a medium-term (three- to five-year) economic policy
program is needed, aimed at developing the main in-
stitutional preconditions for financial stabilization and
economic growth. This program should include the fol-
lowing:

a) a package of microeconomic policy measures, includ-
ing legislative measures (demonopolization and regula-
tion of natural monopolies, stimulation of competition
in the service sphere and industry, regulation of oligop-
oly markets, consumer policy);

b) a package of measures aimed at institutional transfor-
mations in the economy (the setting up of an institution
of effective property owners and the development of the
stock market, the institution of development banks, and
markets in real estate, land, and labor);

C) systemic changes in legislation on investments, fiscal
affairs, and the use of natural resources (the adoption
and elaboration of the following important legislative
acts: "On Securities"; "On Amendments and Additions
to the Law ‘On Foreign Investments’"; "On Conces-
sions and Other Contracts Concluded With Investors";
amendments and additions to the "Fundamentals of Leg-
islation on the Tax System in the Russian Federation";
the "Tax Code"; amendments and additions to the Law
"On Customs Tariffs" and to the "Customs Code"; "On
Agreements on the Division of Output"; amendments
and additions to the Law "On Natural Resources"; the
Law "On Oil and Gas"; the Law "On the Continental
Shelf");

d) stimulation of export-oriented sectors of the econ-
omy and a cautious protectionist policy (through the
stimulation of exports and in particular the credit policy
of development banks, Russia’s defense of its position
on opening up foreign markets to competitive Russian
goods at talks on entering the GATT, the ratification of
a treaty adhering to the European Energy Charter, and a
foreign policy aimed at expanding the market for arms
sales);

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 4s

e) a monetary and budget policy and an exchange rate
policy designed to promote the said measures (this pol-
icy must steer between Scylla and Charybdis — be-
tween keeping inflation no higher than the "background
level" and stimulating overall demand in the context of
an economy undergoing institutional and structural re-
structuring).

Here attention should be focused on the ultimate ob-
jective — an increase in the share of processing indus-
try and high-tech sectors in Russia’s gross product, and
the winning of markets in this connection. Strategically,
this task should be tackled over several decades, and the
funds for tackling it should be redistributed from raw
materials sectors of the economy.

The need to supplement a monetary anti-inflation policy
with a structural and institutional policy is becoming ab-
solutely imperative, not only economically but also po-
litically. Attempts to tackle inflation through a restrictive
monetary policy leave their mark on the economy. Each
such attempt is a setback for the economy: The level of
industrial production and the general level of prosperity
falls.

Prospects

A prediction of the results of the struggle against
inflation in 1995 cannot be reassuring.

In view of the experience of recent years, another
"retreat" can be expected in late summer or early
autumn. The main pro-inflation factors will be as
follows:

— the seasonal increase in budget expenditure, plus the
need to finance "postponed" expenditure from the first
half of 1995;

— the second-quarter increase in the rate of growth
of the money supply (to approximately the average
monthly level for 1994), which was a consequence of
significant ruble interventions by the Bank of Russia in
the currency market

— inflationary expectations in connection with the
absolutely inevitable stage of an accelerated fall in the
ruble exchange rate, since the tendency to strengthen the
exchange rate, which is inappropriate to the situation
(and has already turned into a deliberate game of
“pushing it up” on the part of the major banks), will
certainly give way to the opposite tendency (let us hope
that this will at least be smoother than last year). In this
respect the government’s election campaign will also
have an inevitable negative effect.

Consequently the monthly inflation rate will again reach
double figures — around 10-12 percent by the end of

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

50 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

the year. The situation will again be back virtually to
square one.

The defaults problem will be somewhat alleviated; the
slackening of monetary policy will "enliven" industrial
production. But today a depressive influence is exerted
on the industrial dynamic not only by the degree of
rigor in monetary policy, but also by the dangerously
expanding market in state securities, which draws away
from the financial market more and more money that
is a potentially important investment resource (to a
certain extent the investment slump in the first quarter
of 1995, which was profound — by nearly one-fourth
compared with the same period last year — can be
attributed to this). The government and the Bank of
Russia also underestimate the consequences of their
own anti-inflation currency policy: In the second quarter
alone, as a result of the rise in the ruble exchange rate
provoked by the Bank of Russia, the real dollar rate
on the domestic Russian market fell 26 percent, and
the price competitiveness of Russian goods (including
exports) fell by the same amount.

The establishment of a "band" of permissible currency
fluctuation in the third quarter within the limits of
4,300-4,500 rubles to the dollar may be welcomed as
a step toward ensuring at least some predictability in
the behavior of the main macroeconomic indicators.
However, the fulfillment of this special directive will
mean a further reduction in the real dollar rate (and
therefore in the competitiveness of Russian goods) by,
according to our estimates, a further 5-10 percentage
points. Given such special directives, currency policy
very soon will become a major factor in further reducing
industrial production. The operation of the above two
factors will mean, in our view, that in the second
half of 1995 — despite even the predicted "retreat"
in the policy of financial stabilization — indicators of
industrial dynamics will be worse than in the first half-
year.

Of course, one could be mistaken about the time scale
— concrete steps by the government are unpredictable;
however, the general projection of economic movement
will almost certainly coincide with this prediction.
It is time to learn our lessons and stop swinging
between inflation and industrial recession, alleviating
one problem by exacerbating another.

.4M2107143695 Moscow RABOCHAYA TRIBUNA

{Report by Yuriy Izmaylov: “Duplication Mot Needed

([FBIS Translated Text] "Prior to 1992, oil and gas
equipment was manufactured at only two former USSR
Ministry of the Defense Industry plants — the ‘Bar-
rikady’ Production Association and the Lenin Machin-
ery Plant in Perm. Today Russia’s oil and gas workers
are supplied with equipment from around 100 ‘defense
sector’ enterprises. In the past two or three years, the
sector has assimilated a full range of oil and gas equip-
ment that was previously produced at traditional oil ma-
chine building enterprises," "Soyuzneftemash" Director
Aleksandr Romanikhin said.

As an example he quoted the "Votkinskiy Zavod," the
"Izhevskiy Elektromekhanicheskiy Zavod," the "Bum-
mash" Joint-Stock Company, and the "Izhneftemash"
Joint-Stock Company in Udmurtia. Competitive oil and
gas equipment is also produced by defense enterprises in
Yekaterinburg, St. Petersburg, Perm, Chelyabinsk, and
Tula.

[Izmaylov] But, despite this, imports of equipment for
the oil and gas complex have reached the record level
of $4.5 billion. What is going on?

{[Romanikhin] The state programs "Equipment for the
Fuel and Energy Complex," “Conversion for the Fuel
and Energy Complex," and “Import Substitution in the
Fuel and Energy Complex" were, in my view, drawn up
without seriously analyzing the demand for oil and gas
equipment.

Often after manufacturing a prototype piece of oil
and gas equipment, a defense-sector enterprise would
discover that similar equipment was already being
series-produced by another plant. State resources were
pointlessly wasted, and the military-industrial complex
entered the sectoral market with equipment that was not
in demand.

The “Lukoil” oil company is now channeling money
into the production of only those types of equipment

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

which are in steady demand. "Lukoil" has independently
begun producing a number of important units without
using any budget funds.

Oil and gas workers should realize that the current
craze for imports is a temporary phenomenon. Military-
industrial complex enterprises will inevitably become
the main suppliers of competitive equipment for their
sector.

[Izmayiov] Clearly, budget funding is not adequate to
expand production of oil and gas equipment by military-
industrial complex enterprises. Do we need to raise
funds from Russian and foreign investors?

{[Romanikhin] More than that — it would be expedient

ment for the oil and gas complex into financial-industrial

in Russian 21] Jul95 p4

{Report by Larisa Beloivan: "Good Prospects for Mar-
itime Kray Miners"}

[FBIS Translated Text] Vladivostok — Yuriy Malyshev,
general director of the "Rosugol" concern, and Maritime

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 51

Kray Governor Yevgeniy Nazdratenko have signed an
agreement "On the Development of the Maritime Kray
Coal Industry in 1995-2000."

Under the document, the closest attention will be paid
to the planned development of 14 small open cuts.
"Rosugol" will assume responsibility for delivering new
mining and mine transportation equipment to the kray
to replace dilapidated and obsolete equipment. The kray
administration pledges to provide financial aid for the
construction of small open cuts and the laying of roads
to them. The Maritime Kray Government expressed
readiness to petition the president and the Russian
State Duma to exempt "Primorskugol" and the "Malyye
Razrezy" Joint-Stock Company from taxes, fines, and
customs duty on the imported equipment.

Addressing "Primorskugol" Joint-Stock Company share-
holders, Yuriy Malyshev stated that the kray’s coal in-
dustry has excellent prospects, but currently needs sup-
port. In his opinion, the Maritime Kray miners are
in a unique position. "Whereas most of the country’s
coal-mining enterprises are being stifled by their excess
stocks of output, the Maritime Kray miners have a huge
market and a lack of coal," he said. "If the kray’s coal
industry develops to plan, in three or four years it will
be able to fully meet Maritime Kray’s solid-fuel require-
ments and be able to supply Khabarovsk Kray as well."

of mountains and forests. The Serbian and Croatian
troops have extensive experience of combat operations
in these conditions, therefore, we believe the use of
heavy air strikes will be ineffective. Besides, the Serbian
troops have powerful air defenses and there will be no
possibility of flying at low level. It will put the local
population in danger, who have suffered enough, as well
as the peacekeepers themselves. [end recording]

General of the NSA to our INFOBANK correspondent.
Similar negotiations have already taken place with the
European and the French space agencies. At the mo-
ment, Oleksandr Nehoda, Director General of the NSA,
conducts the second round of negotiations in Washing-
ton, D.C., with Catherine Novelie, a representative of
the Department of Trade in charge with the US space
program. Upon this very lady depends, says Valeriy Ko-
marov, whether Ukraine will be admitted to the Amer-
ican space technologies market. According to Mr. Ko-
marov, the Ukrainian space program depends upon the
consent of the United States to purchase Zenith space
rockets. The commercial launches of Zenith rockets
were discussed during the March 1995 negotiations with
the US Trade Mission in Kiev.

Zenith space rocket, made by the Dnipropetrovsk-based
Pivdenmash, is a two-stage rocket capable to put up
to 14 tons of useful load into the middle orbit. Here
lies its basic drawback: Zenith is unable to put satellites
into the high orbit along the equator line (spacecraft
launched into this orbit "hang" above the territory of
the country which had performed a launch). Ukraine
continues to design three-stage Zenith rocket, which will
enable satellite to reach high geostationary orbit. But
Zenith has two advantages over the foreign analogues:
fully automatized start of this rocket and the use of

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

environmentally clean substances as fuel components,
namely oxygen and kerosene. As Zenith rocket has
the similar characteristics to the French Ariane-4 and
Ariane-5 rockets, it might potentially pose a major
competition to French carriers.

Kuchma’s presidency Ukraine has made a breakthrough
in its relations "with the majority of the influential coun-

organizations," head of the republic’s foreign Ministry
Hennadiy Udovenko declared in an exclusive interview
with INTERFAX.

In his words, development of relations with the United
States, Germany, Canada and deepening of cooperation
with the European Union, NATO, the U.N. and other
Organizations testify to the fact that “Ukraine has
become an important factor on the international arena."

"Over a short period of time there was created an
image of Ukraine as an important European country,
a reliable foreign partner with which is it worth and
necessary to deal," Udovenko said. "This has become
possible primarily thanks to the adoption of the program
of market reforms and to Ukraine’s joining the NPT
{Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty] Treaty."

"These transformations," Udovenko said, “have also
changed the attitude of the international financial struc-
tures to Ukraine, in particular, of G-7 and of the Eu-
ropean Union." "At present the issue on attracting for-
eign investments in the country is on today’s agenda”,
Udovenko noted.

He also said that Ukraine “has stepped the way of sta-
bilization of its relations with Russia and of improving
mutually beneficial relations with CIS countries."

He underlined that “development of partnership with
Russia and CIS countries takes place under diminishing
activity on the western direction." “Ukraine uses all
available opportunities for an equal integration in the
European economic, political, and other structures,"
Udovenko said.

‘Text’ of Foreign Minister’s 21 Jul Speech

WS2407083895 Kiev UKRINFORM in English
0653 GMT 24 Jul 95

{"Text" of statement by Foreign Minister Hennadiy
Udovenko in London on 21 July; from the "Current
Events” feature]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Mr. Chairman, first I would
like to join those colleagues who commanded Prime
Minister John Major for convening this conference.
As a troop-contributing country Ukraine is particularly
interested in participation in this meeting. The Ukrainian
delegation suggests to institutionalize such meetings.
Perhaps, we can name them “the London Group,” or
“John Major Group" or “Malcolm Rifkind Group.”

Second, peace-keeping operation in Bosnia and Herze-
govina is at the crossroads. Both conflicting parties hu-
miliate and harass the UN personnel and do not listen
to the appeals of the international community. We con-
demn both conflicting parties for the use of the language
of ultimatums in relations with UNPROFOR. Under
these circumstance the question of the possible with-
drawal of the UN forces is a legitimate one. But will
the withdrawal of UNPROFOR resolve all the issues
and bring this long-suffering land to peace? But who
will give positive answer to this question? I am sure
that nobody of those present here is ready and willing
to do so. If UNPROFOR is out, who will alleviate the
consequences of the war, notably through assistance in
providing humanitarian aid? Who wiil contain the con-
flict and mitigate its consequences? Who will prevent
the spillover of the conflict to the territories of neighbor-
ing countries? There are no answers to these questions.
That is way the delegation of Ukraine shares the view
put forward by many delegations that the peacekeepers
should stay in Bosnia and Herzegovina.

Third, at the same time we consider it essential that
achieving peace in the region demands UNPROFOR’s
employment and peace-keeping or even peace-enforcing
efforts can deter the escalation of violence. Evidently,
peace cannot be imposed on the parties which don't
want to be helped. Therefore we suggest that this meet-
ing initiates new direct dialogue between the conflict-
ing parties without prejudice to the Contact group plan.
These new negotiations should involve all the conflict-
ing parties (according to the formula) 3+2 and should
aim at the immediate cease-fire, freezing the status quo
and the end of the hostilities. These negotiations could
be conducted with the assistance of a mediator or me-
diator body acceptable to the parties. Ukraine is ready
to provide the venue for such a meeting. We think that
Ukraine — the country which has no specific affiliations
— can be a proper place for holding such a meeting.

UKRAINE 55

Fourth, the main purpose of the current meeting is to
decide how far can we roll back troops under military
pressure «f the conflicting parties. Let me elaborate on
this issue since Ukrainian troops are in the middle of
these tragic events "sandwiched" between advancing
Serbs and defending Muslims. According to latest
information of Associated Press I have just received,
our troops in Zepa have been threatened by both sides.
Serbs previously threatened to attack them, if NATO
planes were called in, and Bosnia Government Forces
threatened to use them as “human shields." That is why
we suggest to undertake immediate operational action to
defend the lives of 79 Ukrainian peacekeepers, who are
there. Srebrenica as a “safe area" has been already lost.
To try to reestablish it would require the adoption of the
new mandate for UNPROFOR under Chapter VII of the
UN Charter. But this would definitely mean the losses
of human lives, and the result of such an operation is
vague. Up to now UNPROFOR simply has no military
and material resources to conduct such operations.

Let me now mention the possible role of Rapid Reaction
Force. The concentration of the combat-equipped Rapid
Reaction Force in one place can prevent the fall of a
“safe area,” but would never resolve the problem of its
protection. With this in mind, we would like to suggest
immediate demilitarization of Gorazde with the explicit
obligations on the part of the conflicting parties to stay
out of there. As far as Sarajevo is concerned, we support
the necessity to reinforce its protection and establish a
relief corridor through Mount Igman — the territory
not controlled by either of the conflicting parties.
The protection of Sarajevo and reestablishment of the
routes of the humanitarian supply will demonstrate
to the Bosnian Serb leaders the determination of the
international community to deter the aggressive actions.

Fifth, besides all this, we think that the time has come
to study the option of lifting sanctions against Belgrade
in order to use its influence on Bosnian Serbs.

Sixth, finally, I would like to inform you that Ukraine
is ready to nominate our special representative to
participate in the expanded Contact Group activities.
Thank you.

the funeral of Volodymyr Romanyuk, patriarch of

Political Direction of New Ukraine Gauged

954F2752A Kiev KIYEVSKIYE VEDOMOSTI
in Russian 6 Jul 95 p 3

[Article by Vladimir Katsman: "‘New Ukraine’ Turns
Away From Federalism and Bilingualism"]

(FBIS Translated Text] The Fourth Congress of the so-
ciopolitical association New Ukraine has changed its po-
litical direction. Now program documents state that New
Ukraine advocates a unitarian structuring of the state
and acknowledges Ukrainian alone as the state language.
At a post-congress press conference, newly elected
chairman of the association Yevgeniy Kushnarev, who
is the mayor of Kharkov and president of the Associa-
tion of Chairmen of City Councils, unambiguously let it
be known that by autumn, when the Party of Democratic
Revival of Ukraine [PDVU] and the Labor Congress of
Ukraine [TKU] complete the unification process within
New Ukraine, the association will have acquired a "pow-
erful political nucleus enabling it to move toward the
creation of a new political structure." Considering the
names of those who have jointed the ruling organs of
New Ukraine (members of the association’s presidium,
for example, include the deputy speaker of parliament,
Oleg Demin; first assistant to the Ukrainian president
Aleksandr Razumkov; presidential adviser on macroe-
conomic issues Aleksandr Galchinskiy; Minister of the
Cabinet of Ministers Valeriy Pustovoytenko; acting First
Deputy Prime Minister Viktor Pynzenyk; State Property
Fund Chairman Yuriy Yekhanurov; and others), we may
assume that the outlines of the "party of power" have
today already appeared. In any case, it is no easy task
to separate these names from official policy.

The leaders of New Ukraine (Yevgeniy Kushnarev’s
deputies are Vladimir Filenko of the PDVU, and Ana-
toliy Matviyenko of the TKU) have stated that while
they support the president’s course of reforms, they
are nonetheless in no hurry to officially appoint "New
Ukrainians” to the government and will make every ef-
fort to caution their people in the leadership structures
(if such turns out to be the case) against a shift to the
left.

As far as the prospect of New Ukraine uniting in
a bloc with other centrist parties is concerned, alas,
not even the outlines of such a possibility are as

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

58 UKRAINE

yet apparent. It is clear that New Ukraine (or more
accurately, the TKU-PDVU party bloc) is closer to
the Social-Democratic wing of centrists. But “classic”
Social-Democrats in Ukraine cannot agree on which
party is the most authentic, and the Social-Democratic
Party of Ukraine is therefore not presently viewed as a
potential bloc partner. Under these circumstances, the
Liberal Party is acting more like a "soft" opposition to
the renewed association. The situation with respect to
the Interregional Reform Bloc [MBR] is more involved.
Twenty-one MBR representatives participated in the
New Ukraine congress as delegates. However, insofar
as the new charter prohibits regional groups from bloc
affiliation with a structure that does not belong to the
association on the all-Ukraine level, most likely there
will be no bloc formed with the MBR, either. Though
Yevgeniy Kushnarev himself has stated repeatedly that
New Ukraine, despite differences in certain program
orientations, is prepared to cooperate with the party
of Vladimir Grinev as far as support for reforms is
concerned. On the eve of the congress, as we know,
the Constitutional Democrats left New Ukraine, and
the Liberal-Democrats who remained failed to obtain
representation in the new New Ukraine leadership
(Liberal Democratic Party of Ukraine leader Vladimir
Klimchuk was literally just a few votes short of election
to the presidium).

But when it comes time for the party slates (the law
will evidently still be based on the proportional-majority
principle), when parties will have to be concerned
about quantitative election-ballot indices, the new New
Ukraine will be forced, along with the others, to solve
the problem of selecting a political partner. If a gal is
too fussy, she may find herself a spinster.... In any case,
New Ukraine has not yet had any positive experience
when it comes to participating in elections...

{[FBIS Translated Text] The Chernobyl Atomic Elec-

UKRAINE 59

been undergoing scheduled preventive maintenance was
put on the nominal load of 1,000 megawatts. Accord-

the “Chernobyl AES" production organization, all the

nological resources and financed in good time from its

of the five Ukrainian nuclear plants that operates at
full capacity. This year, the coefficient of using the
capacity of the third power unit has reached 98.9
percent, whereas the average index for the industry is
84.7 percent. The Chernobyl! AES also leads in adhering
to rules and norms of safe operation.

Economic Issues

National Bank Official Views Relations With IMF

WS2407112095 Kiev INTELNEWS in English
2254 GMT 23 Jul 95

[From the “Economic Review" feature: Interview with
National Bank of Ukraine Foreign Department Chair-
man Oleh Rybachuk by Fedir Storozhenko; date and
place not given]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] [ECONOMIC REVIEW] What
was the reaction of International Monetary Fund (IMF),
Ukraine’s major creditor, to Ukraine’s reforms so far?

{[Rybachuk] Basically, the IMF reaction was predictable.
It was in the form of official documents. First, there
was the letter to President Leonid Kuchma, signed
by Oleh Havrylyshyn, deputy chairman of the group
Ukraine participates in. The second letter is to the prime
minister, chief of staff of the president and deputy prime
ministers.

The spirit of the letters is the same; they stress that
at the last IMF Board of Directors meeting they were
delighted by the reformation process in Ukraine and that
the decision on the second tranche of the stand-by credit
was nearly unanimous. This impression was retained up
to now, although there were certain problems, as non-
payments for gas, slowness of the privatization process
and exceeding liquidity of the National Bank of Ukraine
(NBU) — the amount of money in circulation increased
but the NBU issued deposit certificates to bind the extra
resources.

The greatest unrest and misunderstanding were caused
by the announcements of Ukrainian officials on retain-
ing the current inflation rate until the end of the year.
This kind of information makes the provision of the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

60 UKRAINE

third tranche by the Board of Directors to Ukraine very
problematic and this was put in a very rigid form.

[ECONOMIC REVIEW] What caused the unrest of the
IMF?

[Rybachuk] This can be looked at from such a view-
point: Financial aid to Ukraine’s program has been
signed recently — this spring — and is now active.
We have not accomplished even the half of the pro-
gram but we started to correct it. Things like these are
not favored but when the backbone of the program is
changed, the situation becomes very serious. There are
attempts to change the anti-inflation policy and it is the
foundation for everything.

The program could stand alteration of the privatization
schedule but as we can see now, it will not be
accomplished in time. But the optimistic thing is that the
State Property Fund became a branch of the executive
body and is subordinated to the government. But the
anti-inflation policy is the backbone of the program and
there will be no bargaining between Ukrainian officials
and the IMF.

[ECONOMIC REVIEW] Is it possible Ukrainian offi-
cials did not consult with the IMF on changes in the
economic policy?

[Rybachuk] It caused the IMF’s misunderstanding and
even irritation that the announcement was done in the
mass media and that no consulting with Gram Justice,
IMF representative in Kiev or with Washington office
staff was conducted at all. So, now a lot depends upon
conclusions of the IMF mission to come to Kiev for
reformation analysis.

By the way, the success of this mission might have
influence on the attitude of Stanley Fisher, IMF vice
president, a famous American economist. He pays
great attention to Ukraine, as shown in Madrid. The
Ukrainian reforms program was subject to his personal
concern. But if he comes to Ukraine, it will happen
only in reassurance that there will be no changes in
the economical reforms program, especially in monetary
and anti-inflation policies.

[ECONOMIC REVIEW] What happens if the results of
mission’s work are negative?

[Rybachuk] It depends on the measures the government
will take. In the short term, there will be the meeting
of ambassadors of Ukraine’s donor countries in the
IMF, as the donors have shown the greatest reaction
and started to appeal to IMF representative office in
Kiev, requesting an explanation for announcements of
Ukrainian officials.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

So, this meeting has to resolve many questions. But,
generally, the success or failure of the IMF mission
might raise the question on continuation or cancellation
of funding for Ukraine’s economic reform. And in this
case, not only IMF credits but also credits of the World
Bank, European Union and Japan are concerned as they
are bound together. I wouldn’t continue because the
probiem is too serious.

[ECONOMIC REVIEW] What is the IMF participation
in the overall aid to Ukraine?

{[Rybachuk] It is becoming obvious that funding for
Ukrainian economics by the West on the two-sided
basis is minimized. The clear indications for that were
shown during the visit of Ukrainian officials to Germany
and during talks with Finance Ministers of the western
countries. All the talk is about funding through the
pool — IMF, the World Bank, the European Bank for
Reconstruction and Development.

[ECONOMIC REVIEW] Does that mean the future of
reforms in Ukraine depends upon results of IMF mission
work?

{[Rybachuk] Of course. It is utopia to talk about suc-
cessful reforms in Ukraine without foreign aid, because
the program package includes a grace period for energy
debts. All this goes together, it is one program.

[ECONOMIC REVIEW] Why did the idea of an
inflation level of 4 to 5 percent a month instead of the
planned tnonthly 1 to 2 percent as a goal by the end of
the year disturb IMF so much?

{[Rybachuk] What all the talk is about, is that you
cannot control inflation at a monthly 4 to 5 percent
as experience shows that inflation can be managed and
controlled at the level of a monthly 0.5 to 1 percent.
If inflation goes beyond that, as is proven by the world
practice, it becomes uncontrollable. This applies to our
4 to 5 percent.

And the worst thing is that in this fashion the trust to the
reforms is being destroyed. There already are indications
that the real income of citizens grew (within last 4 to
5 months), the deposits to commercial banks system
increased and these are signs for financial stabilization,
while financial improvement is the first one to signal
coming stabilization. Then there is the turn of the small
and medium-size business. And there is no sense to
expect production normalization for industrial monsters,
such as heavy machine building.

The greatest threat is that these sprouts will be destroyed
with a reason that we are in economic crisis. This
approach is ideologically unacceptable.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

[ECONOMIC REVIEW] What are further ways you can
suggest improving the situation?

{[Rybachuk] Nearly daily consulting of the institutions
involved in the reforms process — the Economics
Ministry, the Finance Ministry, the National Bank of
Ukraine are held. The problem of the reforms course has
to be solved within Ukraine and after that the official
announcement has to be done that there will be no
deviation from the major trend. This is the only way to
stop the wave of misunderstanding started by the events
mentioned above.

WS2107105395 Kiev HOLOS UKRAYINY

["Text" of address by President L. Kuchma to Ukrainian

{[FBIS Translated Text] Dear compatriots! The harvest
time has come to Ukrainian fields — the hottest
and most important period of the year, crowning and
finalizing the work of many millions of people and
laying the foundation of wealth for the entire nation.

Ukraine has long waited for a bumper crop. Today,

conditions — heat and heavy rains — have changed
preliminary estimates. Nevertheless, in southern and
northern oblasts and in the western and eastern regions,
the fields became rich in the lush and abundant ears.

There was a lot of talk forecasting failure of the sowing
campaign...

These forecasts have proven wrong.

We have managed to sow crops and grow them. |
trust that now, at the last stage of the agricultural
cycle, the grain-growers will show an example of high
responsibility and discipline, conducting the harvest
campaign quickly and thoroughly, ensuring fodder for

UKRAINE 61

cattle-breeding industry, and laying a firm foundation
for the prospective crop.

The president and the government are undertaking
urgent measures to ensure smooth organization for the
1995 harvest, as well as provision of food and fodder
for domestic needs and exports.

Methods of creating the state food reserves have sub-
stantially changed this year, replacing mandatory orders
with contracts. This has given a lot of freedom to man-
ufacturers and made them interested in final results of
their work. Now, they need to take advantage of the
new opportunities.

I have instructed relevant ministries and institutions
and local government bodies to provide the maximum
possible amscunt of funds, credits, fuels and lubricants,
spare parts, and equipment to farms and processing and
procuring plants. Implementation of my instructions will
be rigidly controlled.

Will it be sufficient? No, it will not.

Let me tell you frankly and honestly: For objective
and subjective reasons, the state is presently unable
to give our peasant, our Great Worker, everything
which is necessary for the normal production process,
including adequate living conditions in return for hard
work. Many problems are not yet resolved properly. The
countryside is starved for new equipment, technologies,
and material and financial resources, including basic
consumer products. In some regions, wages are not paid
on time.

The president and the government do their best to
resolve these vital problems, which are experienced by
the countryside. All presently available state resources
are being funneled into this crucial area for the well-
being of the state and citizens. Favorable conditions are
being created for attracting nonstate and foreign funds,
equipment, and modern technology.

Also, we cannot let objective economic conditions,
coupled by ineptness and plain lack of economic skills,
damage the harvest which we have grown with so much
difficulty.

The priority task for all management and economic lev-
els is to ensure a smooth and stable harvest collection
efforts. Carelessness, bureaucracy, and parochial atti-
tudes toward this extremely important state matter are
unacceptable. Power engineering specialists, industrial-
ists, as well as people working in banks, trade, municipal
institutions, customs officers — all of them must know
their place in this crucial matter.

Absolutely zverything should be subordinated to the
harvest ar4 main task — to gather bread on time and

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

62 UKRAINE

without losses so that every wheat ear gets to the state,
collective farms, or farmers’ warehouses.

Special attention should be focused on those participat-
ing in the harvest. Central and local government bod-
ies should ensure most favorable conditions for those
who spend day and night conscientiously working on the
fields. Honest and efficient work has to be adequately
encouraged. The state seeks ways to repay debts it runs
to the countryside and ensure timely and complete set-
tlement of mutual payments for the future harvest. We
no longer have the right to fool the peasant.

Local government institutions and industrial enterprise
leaders should consider providing urban residents with
food supplies by giving them land plots and kitchen
gardens and letting them participate in agricultural
works in collective and other farms. For this purpose,
we need to settle relevant organizational, transportation,
and other problems.

In one of my first public appearances after ! was elected
president, 1 deciared that it is the Ukrainian peasant
who epitomizes the rock bed of political and economic
stability of the state, and that it is his diligence, patience,
and wisdom that hold our society together. Today, I
have become even more convinced of this. That is why I
am appealing to you, the Ukrainian peasants, to conduct
the harvest honestly and thoroughly, preventing losses.

The progress of poiitical, economic, and social transfor-
mations in Ukraine, its present and future, as well as the

Bread and the independence of our state are very close

is also being decided.

Thanks to the titanic efforts of our grain-makers, there

supplies to our population as sooi as this year.

I am appealing to all rural and urban workers and to all

full advantage of the available financial, material, and
human resources to ensure the creation of the richest
possible food reserves in Ukraine.

I trust that our people’s centuries-long diligence and
love of the land will help collect and preserve the grown
harvest.

Thank you!

Legislator Criticizes Central Bank Policy

WS2107125195 Kiev KIYEVSKIYE NOVOSTI
in Russian 14 Jul 95 pp 4,6

("Exclusive interview with Viktor Ivanovych Suslov,
chairman of the parliamentary commission for issues of
finances and banking policy, by Volodymyr Kartashov;
place and date not given: "It Is But One Step From
Bankruptcy to Chaos. Does the National Bank of
Ukraine Know This?"}

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] [Kartashov] Let us begin
our interview with recent events. The Press Service ef
the National Bank of Ukraine [NBU] has reported that
activities of the commercial bank "Ternava" (Ternopil)
and commercial joint stock bank "“Lehprombank"
(Mykolayiv) were halted. The liquidation of these
banks will be executed in accordance with the Statute
of Sanctions Imposed by the NBU for Violating Bank-
ing Legislation. Previously, 11 banks were liquidated.
Thus, out of 228 commercial banks of the new wave,
we have 215 banks left. Due to a tough monetary
policy, administrative pressure by the NBU, and bizarre
taxation, these banks are in a difficult situation, too. In
your opinion, what is the reason for the crisis in the
banking system?

[Suslov] I will begin with a comment regarding the
number of commercial banks. Some people say: "We
are short of banks. The United States has 15,000
banks...." Other people state the opposite — "We have
a lot of banks" — and cite the example of Canada,
which can function with 13 banks. It all depends on
the banking system. Banks in the United States, for
instance, virtually do not have any branches. Other
countries have less banks, but those banks possess a
wide network of branches. Every state should approach
the development of its banking system proceedins from
its specific circumstances.

It should be well understood that the point is not in
the number of banks, but in the knowledge of the fact
that bankruptcy of any bank delivers a powerful blow
to the national economy. The collapse of several large
banks may lead to chaos and ruin the entire financial
and credit system. Bankruptcy of a bank can by no
means be compared to bankruptcy of a state enterprise.
A normal bank has tens of thousands of customers and
enterprises keeping their accounts in it, so its bankruptcy
strikes entire economic sectors. Many banks are not
large in terms of the volume of their operations, but they
may specialize in one or several economic branches.
If such a bank finds itself in a difficult situation, the
future of entire branches may be threatened. Therefore,
it is not accidental that many developed countries pay
great attention to the safety of banking institutions. They

This report may contain copyrighted matenal. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

continually improve their methods for keeping banks
"afloat." I mean such methods as insurance, compulsory
readjustment, and bank mergers.

[Kartashov] We only talk about this, but do nothing to
improve the situation. Are we working on such methods
at least theoretically?

[Suslov] Indeed, we have not yet gone so far. What is
wrong with our policy regarding commercial banks? In
my opinion, the wrong is in our totalitarian mentality, in
the lack of market-oriented psychology on the part of a
considerable body of citizens, including those “clutching
power for themselves." Due to this, bankruptcy of a
bank evokes a malicious smile from many: "Now you
have done it with your credits!" Not only that specific
bank, but its customers also "have done it.” This means
woe and grief to hundreds of thousands of people. As
soon as a bank finds itself in such a situation, we in
the Supreme Council receive a gush of appeals and
statements by those having lost their deposits. Each
bank has thousands of depositors. Nobody can help
them compensate their losses. Does it follow that they
also have done it? Both the National Bank and the
Supreme Council can equally be reproached for not yet
resolving the issue of the safety of banking institutions
and introducing an appropriate supervision system.

[Kartashov] What is your commission doing to try to
change this situation?

[Suslov] We think that it is necessary to do everything
possible to protect the interests of depositors. We
are currently working out a draft law on insuring
deposits, according to which bank depositors will be
protected if their bank goes bankrupt. Naturaily, it
is necessary to create an appropriate insurance fund
for this purpose. But this is not the main road. The
main road, of course, is to elaborate and legislatively
seal a system for readjusting banks, including their
compulsory merger or state support if they comply to
certain conditions. President Leonid Kuchma, in his
speech in Uzhhorod, said that he does not rule out the
possibility of nationalizing banks. In my opinion, such
an approach cannot be the basis of state policy.

What does the nationalization of a bank mean? It
primarily means that in such a case the state assumes
all debts and settles all liabilities of a given bank.
How can Ukraine, with its meager budget, find such
money? I think that such an option is possible only
in exceptional cases, where it is necessary to protect
serious economic interests concentrated in a given bank.
I am assured that the closure of any bank should be
treated as an emergency event; if possible, such a
measure should be avoided. It is necessary to follow the
path of tighter supervision of banks. We do not have

UKRAINE 63

such a supervision mechanism today. Let us suppose
that a bank is facing closure or standing on the verge
of bankruptcy. An inquiry as to the reasons reveals that
the bank has deliberately given away the majority of
its resources in the form of grants or loans without
pledge, or has on numerous occasions given credits
to firms that were bankrupt for a long time, etc. In
other words, its activities were of a criminal nature.
Such “mini banks," operating with criminal methods,
are numerous. However, if they are able to operate and
flourish for some time, then this only testifies to the fact
that the level of supervision by the National Bank is far
from satisfactory. Our commission, after examining this
issue, has even demanded that the National Bank make
personal changes in the composition of its board.

[Kartashov] What were the NBU’s conclusions?

[Suslov] NBU Board Deputy Chairman Markov, who
was responsible for this area of work, has been fired. A
new man to this post has not yet been appointed, or, to
be precise, has not yet been introduced to us, since the
members of the NBU Board have to be approved by the
Supreme Council Presidium.

[Kartashov] As of today, even NBU Board Chairman
Viktor Yushchenko is being subjected to quite sharp
criticism...

[Suslov] You are right. Such criticism was once again
voiced in a report by President Kuchma in connection
with the NBU’s tough monetary policy. Both in the
Supreme Council and at his news conferences, Leonid
Kuchma is urging a softer monetary course. I think that
he is right.

{Kartashov] It follows that the NBU should abandon its
policy of tough monetarism?

[Suslov] Such is the president’s stance. It is clear that
not everybody has fully understood how strongly the
situation has changed in connection with the signing of
the Constitutional Agreement. First, the NBU is now
deprived of legislative initiative. This means that any
project submitted by the NBU to the Supreme Council
has to be sent by us for review and approval to the gov-
ernment or the presidential administration. Sometimes
it has to be sent to both institutions simultaneously.
Second, indicators of objective policy [indikatory pred-
metnoy politiki] are being determined by the president.
This is probably why the Supreme Council has not yet
approved the “Main Directions of Credit and Financial
Policy for 1995." This document was submitted to us by
the NBU, but since it was not agreed to with the gov-
ernment, we sent it to the Cabinet of Ministers. We have
not yet received an answer. Therefore, we do not know

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

64 UKRAINE

whether the government agrees with such a policy. Fur-
thermore, according to the Constitutional Agreement,
the chairman of the NBU Board, as is the case with the
Prosecutor General, is proposed by the president and
approved by the Supreme Council. The same procedure
is applied to dismissals from this post. This stipulation
of the Constitutional Agreement is currently subject to
mixed interpretations. Some people think that the pres-
ident should now make use of this right and propose
a new candidate for the post of NBU chairman to be
examined by the Supreme Council. Others see this in
a different way. Everything will become clear after the
so-called "case" of the Prosecutor General [Datsyuk] is
solved.

In the long run, however, it all comes to the requirement
that the NBU should bring its policy in line with
that pursued by the president. This is absolutely right.
Following the signing of the Constitutional Agreement,
the role of the NBU has noticeably diminished — the
NBU is to be, if one may say so, a pressing machine of
the executive power personified by the president and the
Cabinet of Ministers. The NBU today is simply obliged
to coordinate its policy with the executive. Such are
the requirements of the interim period, in which our
country has found itself. If the NBU does not do this,
the president, in my opinion, will surely make use of his

right. He will not criticize the NBU’s policy anymore,
but will resort to administrative levers of control.

{[Kartashov] However, it is still possible to assert that
for the time being the NBU is playing the role of a mo-
nopolist. The NBU is not appropriately counterbalanced
Therefore, it may be necessary to create an appropriate
supervisory body for the banking sphere.. As tar as |
am informed, there exist two projects for creating such
a body. One project was proposed by the Kievan Bank-
ing Union, the other is a component of the Law on the
National Bank of Ukraine, which has been drafted by a
group of people’s deputies. What is your attitude toward
the creation of such a body?

[Suslov] My: answer is in the affirmative. The Ukrainian
president thinks that such a body should operate inde-
pendently from the NBU. The NBU opposes this view.
The draft law on the NBU, which you mentioned, stip-
ulates that the supervisory body should be in charge of
the NBU. This is a very important issue, and we, the
legislators, need to examine it in the most considerate
way. It is we who will determine to a significant extent
to whom this body should be subordinated.

{[Kartashov] What is your personal point of view?

[Suslov] There are pros and cons here. Today, many of
NBU’s functions are concentrated. This is understand-
able, since the NBU is a direct successor to the [former]

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

State Bank. The State Bank had to perform all functions,
since there were no other banks. The NBU is gradually
getting rid of these functions, but it still retains many
that should not be performed by the central bank. In
Germany, for example, the department for supervising
banks is not included in the structure of the Bundesbank.
It is an independent body. The central bank in Germany
is primarily concerned with implementing financial and
credit policy and securing the stability of the monetary
unit. This is why the Bundesbank is supervising com-
mercial banks. [as published] In future, we will surely
come to such a situation, too, but this in no way means
that we should pursue such a policy today.

What is wrong with our current banking system? It is
the fact that the system draws a definite dividing line
between the rights of commercial banks and those of
State institutions. We are currently arguing with the
NBU over one elementary question: Is the NBU a state
institution or not? The NBU Board persistently protects
its state status, regardless of how paradoxical it may
seem. In such a case, the NBU’s employees’ salaries
should be the same as other state servants, and the NBU
should operate according to the laws governing state
institutions.

{Kartashov] But this, as of today, is not advantageous?

{[Suslov] It is not. On the other hand, it is also
inadmissible to commercialize the NBU, because the
NBU, due to its nature, is a state institution. The NBU
wants to be somewhere in between....

[Kartashov] Viktor Ivanovych, the details presented by
you testify to the fact that the NBU has not so far
worked out a sensible mechanism for regulating the
activity of commercial banks. What mechanism would
you propose?

[Suslov] The main thing is that everything related to
the issue of regulating the activity of commercial banks
should be supported by legislative acts. Unfortunately,
we do not get any assistance from the NBU on this
matter. The problem lies in the fact that today the NBU
regulates the activity of commercial banks by telegrams
of every sort, which are not even signed by the the
NBU Board chairman. Banks are often nonplussed by
contradictions in these telegrams. As a result, not only
the banking system, but the economy as a whole suffers
from this. We should at last determine — clearly and on
the legislative level — both the procedure for registering
commercial banks and the sum of authorized funds,
as well as to delineate the scope of functional activity
of banks. In addition, all norms should obligatorily be
determined by legislative acts. If all this is done on
the legislative level, then it will be easier to work for

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

both the NBU and commercial banks, since stability will
appear.

What is going on at present? Let us suppose that an in-
struction has been issued for commercial banks regard-
ing their deposit reserves. Operations with depositors’
money have been eased, so banks are starting to develop
this kind of service. Afterward, a telegram is unexpect-

UKRAINE 65

edly transmitted with instructions to increase the norms
for reserves deposited by the population. This kind of
service is abruptly becoming unprofitable. Banks, hav-
ing spent huge sums, suffer a loss, as well as refuse
to receive deposits and pay interest on deposits. Banks
become feverish.... Such things happen systematically.
[passage omitted]

Belarus

Lukashenka: Orthodox Christianity ‘Moral
Necessity’

LD2207173895 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1501 GMT 22 Jul 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] MINSK, July 22 (INTERFAX-
WEST) — President Alyaksandr Lukashenka of Belarus
said on Saturday it was not a political but a moral
necessity to preserve and develop Orthodox Christianity
in Belarus, Russia and Ukraine.

"We have no right to cut the branch on which we are
sitting,” Lukashenka said as he welcomed the visiting
head of the Russian Orthodox Church, Patriarch Aleksey
Il. "If we lose, through our own unwise actions, Slav
culture as our foundation, we will thereby encourage
spiritual and ideological aggression.”

The president, who received Aleksey in his residence
in Minsk, deplored today’s disunity within the Russian
Orthodox Church. "Splits and separatism in Orthodoxy
cannot be tolerated,” he said.

Roman Catholics and Protestants in Belarus “have
embarked on a path close to political intrigue," he
went on. "Something has started that resembles sporting
contests for the opening of Catholic churches and
(Protestant) prayer centers. That is an insult to us,
because we have never raised any obstacles to anyone.
One is getting the impression that a breeding ground for
future conflicts is taking shape.”

Lukashenka expressed the hope that Aleksey’s planned
five-day stay in Belarus would “sober up the hot heads
of those who wish to see the people of Belarus lose their
identity and their pride.”

The Russian Orthodox Church “has been and remains
loyal to its principles and is not trying to fish in the
troubled waters of interparty disputes,” the Belarussian
leader said.

"We have great respect for the Orthodox Church, which
takes an active part in social life, in the overcoming of
ethnic conflicts and confrontations and in the work to
eliminate the consequences of the Chernobyl! accident
(a 1986 accident at Ukraine’s Chernobyl nuclear power
plant which also badly affected neighboring Belarus).”

Lukashenka also said he hoped the Orthodox Church
would always share the "joys and difficulties” of the
Belarussian people and help the republic solve its
problems.

There are today 918 Orthodox parishes in Belarus
and in the last five years 318 new parishes and eight
monasteries have been opened, the president said.

FBIS-SOV-95-141
24 July 1995

The Russian Orthodox Church also has a seminary and
a religious school in Minsk and will soon have an
academy in Belarus. There are also 136 Sunday schools
in the country.

omy of Gagauzia can serve as a precedent for resolving
the problem of Transdniester, President Mircea Snegur
said after a two-day tour of southern parts of Moldova.

He described the situation in Gagauzia as stable.

Snegur said Chisinau must do its utmost to help towards
economic recovery of the territory. In his words, huge
spending will be needed to recommission local food
processing facilities and create new ones.

Snegur spoke of a recent meeting with Turkish President
Suleyman Demirel in Bucharest at which Demirel had
reaffirmed his country’s willingness to provide a $35
million loan to Moldova. Snegur said most of this would
be used to help resolve Gagauzia’s problems.

WESTERN REGION 67

Today marks exactly three years since Russia and
Moldova signed an agreement on settling the armed
conflict in Transdniester.

A joint statement by the co-chairmen of the peace
monitoring commission of Moldova, Transdniester and
Russia made available to INTERFAX says that the
peacemaking operation in the region proved effective
and was the optimal way of putting an end to the
military stand off at the time.

It speaks of a seizure of large amounts of weapons over
the period and clearing vast areas of land from mines.
Positive results are also reported about police operations
and searches of those unaccounted for.

For his part, leader of the self-styled Transdniestrian re-
public Igor Smirnov told Interfax that nothing short of
the region’s statehood can serve as a guarantor of the
peace accords signed by Chisinau and Tiraspol. In his
words, ongoing talks with Chisinau are aimed at con-
solidating and recognition of Transdniester’s statehood.

However, Smirnov said Moldova had rejected Tiraspol’s
latest proposal that the two sides sign a memorandum
on the region’s statehood.

Nonetheless, he called for further talks with Chisinau
with possible Ukrainian involvement. Smirnov said
Kiev may appoint its own envoy as Russia did.

However, he is firmly opposed to Romania’s participa-
tion saying Bucharest "has always stalled the negotiating
process."

20-Year Lease of Leninsk to Russia Criticized

LD2207150895 Almaty GORIZONT in Russian
21-27 Jul 95 p5

{FBIS Translated Excerpt] Literally in a week’s time,
Leninsk (one of the two towns subordinate to the
republic’s government) will stop being Kazakh for 20
years. The agreement on this was initialled by Russian
Deputy Prime Minister Aleksey Bolshakov and First
Deputy Prime Minister of Kazakhstan Nyghmetzhan
Yesengharin.

The high-ranking statesmen explained this decision
as follows: Kazakhstan has handed Baykonur [space-
launch center near the Kazakh town of Leninsk] over to
Russia for a long period. So, they say, the governments
of the two countries also consider it necessary to
put Leninsk (the majority of the population of which
services the cosmodrome) under the jurisdiction of the
Russian Federation.

By the way, the signing of the Baykonur agreement has
hardly been noticed. If one disregards the limp publi-
cations of the Kazakh press and the scant information
in the Russian means media, then one can say that this
historic event did not evoke any specific reaction from
either sides. It is to some extent explicable. The politi-
cians of the neighboring state are much more worried
about such problems as the war in Chechnya and the
forthcoming elections than about “some Leninsk.” There
is a Similar situation in Kazakhstan — the adoption of

CENTRAL ASIA 69

the new constitution is at hand, as is the move to Ak-
mola [the new capital of Kazakhstan]. The public itself,
excluding some radicals, has also refrained from making
comments in public, taking their lead form the powers
that be.

Does this mean that the problem is non-existent? Does
this mean that Leninsk, in reality, must remain on the
verge of global Kazakh-Russian relations? Let us take a
profound look at it, look weightily, try to assess the real
Situation and to calculate all the consequences of the
agreement. For example, the public speeches of some
people give me not the slightest cause for hope but, on
the contrary, make me worry about those who say that
the transfer of Leninsk will supposedly not affect the
State interests of Kazakhstan in the least. I do not know
to whose accompaniment these gentlemen are "singing",
but I will permit myself not to agree with them.

Let’s take a closer look at this. The supporters of
the agreement maintain: up until recently, the town
{Leninsk] was financed by Russia at 94 percent, while
now, they say, Kazakhstan has been freed from the
burden of paying the remaining six percent. Questions
arise immediately. Was is worth creating the problem
because of this ill-fated six percent, if the expenditures
were not at all burdensome? The population of Leninsk
has not exceeded 100,000 people (many of whom
have professional qualifications which are in demand!)
The second question: how much will the ecological
rehabilitation of the area cost the republic after 20 years
of intensive exploitation? The third: does not it seem,
gentlemen opponents, that the introduction of a mass
of [Russian] roubles into Leninsk will undermine the
stability of the tenge? And, fourthly, to whom will the
profits of Baykonur and the enterprises of the defense
departments go? It is perfectly clear that if they [defense
bodies} are unprofitable, then they will be restructured.
At any rate, Leninsk industry will not bring benefit
to Kazakhstan in the foreseeable future. If it is to be
restructured, then the Russian side will remove all the
equipment on the expiry of the terms of the agreement.
If not, then in 20 years the burden of conversion
problems will fall on Kazakhstan.

The political aspects of putting Leninsk under the juris-
diction of the Russian Federation also cause many prob-
lems. It is no accident that before and after concluding
the agreement Russia repeatedly emphasized that the
town would be closed [to outsiders], as it was during
the time of the USSR.

Therefore, space technology will not be accessible to
Kazakh specialists. This is extremely undesirable, as the
space industry, in which Kazakhstan is not obviously
prominent, is considered very up to date.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

70 CENTRAL ASIA

A closed Leninsk has another undesirable aspect. The
West is worried at the strengthening of imperialist
attitudes in Russia, and now can directly or indirectly
count Kazakhstan as one of the strategic partners of the
Russian Federation. The G7 will have yet more grounds
for restricting financial assistance to the Russian bear
and, together with it, to Kazakhstan. [passage omitted]

In general, the Baykonur-Leninsk problem has been
settled legally. But to what extent is this just?
